</div><div class="mw-content-ltr mw-parser-output" lang="en" dir="ltr"><div class="shortdescription nomobile noexcerpt noprint searchaux" style="display:none">Continent</div>
<style data-mw-deduplicate="TemplateStyles:r1320445320">.mw-parser-output .hatnote{font-style:italic}.mw-parser-output div.hatnote{padding-left:1.6em;margin-bottom:0.5em}.mw-parser-output .hatnote i{font-style:normal}.mw-parser-output .hatnote+link+.hatnote{margin-top:-0.5em}@media print{body.ns-0 .mw-parser-output .hatnote{display:none!important}}</style><div role="note" class="hatnote navigation-not-searchable">This article is about the continent. For other uses, see <a href="/wiki/Asia_(disambiguation)" class="mw-disambig" title="Asia (disambiguation)">Asia (disambiguation)</a>.</div>

</div></td></tr><tr><th scope="row" class="infobox-label"><a href="/wiki/UN_M49" title="UN M49">UN M49</a> code</th><td class="infobox-data"><code>142</code> – Asia<br /><code>001</code> – World</td></tr></tbody></table>
<p><b>Asia</b> (<span class="rt-commentedText nowrap"><span class="IPA nopopups noexcerpt" lang="en-fonipa"><a href="/wiki/Help:IPA/English" title="Help:IPA/English">/<span style="border-bottom:1px dotted"><span title="/ˈ/: primary stress follows">ˈ</span><span title="/eɪ/: &#39;a&#39; in &#39;face&#39;">eɪ</span><span title="/ʒ/: &#39;s&#39; in &#39;pleasure&#39;">ʒ</span><span title="/ə/: &#39;a&#39; in &#39;about&#39;">ə</span></span>/</a></span>&#32;<span class="ext-phonos"><span data-nosnippet="" id="ooui-php-1" class="noexcerpt ext-phonos-PhonosButton ext-phonos-PhonosButton-emptylabel oo-ui-widget oo-ui-widget-enabled oo-ui-buttonElement oo-ui-buttonElement-frameless oo-ui-iconElement oo-ui-buttonWidget" data-ooui="{&quot;_&quot;:&quot;mw.Phonos.PhonosButton&quot;,&quot;href&quot;:&quot;\/\/upload.wikimedia.org\/wikipedia\/commons\/transcoded\/2\/2e\/En-us-Asia.ogg\/En-us-Asia.ogg.mp3&quot;,&quot;rel&quot;:[&quot;nofollow&quot;],&quot;framed&quot;:false,&quot;icon&quot;:&quot;volumeUp&quot;,&quot;data&quot;:{&quot;ipa&quot;:&quot;&quot;,&quot;text&quot;:&quot;&quot;,&quot;lang&quot;:&quot;en&quot;,&quot;wikibase&quot;:&quot;&quot;,&quot;file&quot;:&quot;En-us-Asia.ogg&quot;},&quot;classes&quot;:[&quot;noexcerpt&quot;,&quot;ext-phonos-PhonosButton&quot;,&quot;ext-phonos-PhonosButton-emptylabel&quot;]}"><a role="button" tabindex="0" href="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/transcoded/2/2e/En-us-Asia.ogg/En-us-Asia.ogg.mp3" rel="nofollow" aria-label="Play audio" title="Play audio" class="oo-ui-buttonElement-button"><span class="oo-ui-iconElement-icon oo-ui-icon-volumeUp"></span><span class="oo-ui-labelElement-label"></span><span class="oo-ui-indicatorElement-indicator oo-ui-indicatorElement-noIndicator"></span></a></span><sup class="ext-phonos-attribution noexcerpt navigation-not-searchable"><a href="/wiki/File:En-us-Asia.ogg" title="File:En-us-Asia.ogg">ⓘ</a></sup></span></span> <a href="/wiki/Help:Pronunciation_respelling_key" title="Help:Pronunciation respelling key"><i title="English pronunciation respelling"><span style="font-size:90%">AY</span>-zhə</i></a>, <span class="rt-commentedText nowrap"><style data-mw-deduplicate="TemplateStyles:r1177148991">.mw-parser-output .IPA-label-small{font-size:85%}.mw-parser-output .references .IPA-label-small,.mw-parser-output .infobox .IPA-label-small,.mw-parser-output .navbox .IPA-label-small{font-size:100%}</style><span class="IPA-label IPA-label-small"><a href="/wiki/British_English" title="British English">UK</a> also </span><span class="IPA nopopups noexcerpt" lang="en-fonipa"><a href="/wiki/Help:IPA/English" title="Help:IPA/English">/<span style="border-bottom:1px dotted"><span title="/ˈ/: primary stress follows">ˈ</span><span title="/eɪ/: &#39;a&#39; in &#39;face&#39;">eɪ</span><span title="/ʃ/: &#39;sh&#39; in &#39;shy&#39;">ʃ</span><span title="/ə/: &#39;a&#39; in &#39;about&#39;">ə</span></span>/</a></span></span> <a href="/wiki/Help:Pronunciation_respelling_key" title="Help:Pronunciation respelling key"><i title="English pronunciation respelling"><span style="font-size:90%">AY</span>-shə</i></a>) is the largest <a href="/wiki/Continent" title="Continent">continent</a><sup id="cite&#95;ref-10" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-10"><span class="cite-bracket">&#91;</span>note 1<span class="cite-bracket">&#93;</span></a></sup><sup id="cite&#95;ref-11" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-11"><span class="cite-bracket">&#91;</span>10<span class="cite-bracket">&#93;</span></a></sup><sup id="cite&#95;ref-Boudreau&#95;12-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Boudreau-12"><span class="cite-bracket">&#91;</span>11<span class="cite-bracket">&#93;</span></a></sup> in the world by both land area and population.<sup id="cite&#95;ref-Boudreau&#95;12-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Boudreau-12"><span class="cite-bracket">&#91;</span>11<span class="cite-bracket">&#93;</span></a></sup> It covers an area of more than 44 million square kilometres,<sup id="cite&#95;ref-13" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-13"><span class="cite-bracket">&#91;</span>note 2<span class="cite-bracket">&#93;</span></a></sup> about 30% of Earth's total land area and 8% of Earth's total surface area. The continent, which has long been home to the majority of the <a href="/wiki/Human_population" class="mw-redirect" title="Human population">human population</a>,<sup id="cite&#95;ref-14" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-14"><span class="cite-bracket">&#91;</span>12<span class="cite-bracket">&#93;</span></a></sup> was the site of many of the <a href="/wiki/First_civilisations" class="mw-redirect" title="First civilisations">first civilisations</a>. Its 4.7&#160;billion people constitute roughly 60% of the world's population.
</p><p>Asia shares the <a href="/wiki/Landmass" title="Landmass">landmass</a> of <a href="/wiki/Eurasia" title="Eurasia">Eurasia</a> with <a href="/wiki/Europe" title="Europe">Europe</a>, and of <a href="/wiki/Afro-Eurasia" title="Afro-Eurasia">Afro-Eurasia</a> with both Europe and <a href="/wiki/Africa" title="Africa">Africa</a>. In general terms, it is bounded on the east by the <a href="/wiki/Pacific_Ocean" title="Pacific Ocean">Pacific Ocean</a>, on the south by the <a href="/wiki/Indian_Ocean" title="Indian Ocean">Indian Ocean</a>, and on the north by the <a href="/wiki/Arctic_Ocean" title="Arctic Ocean">Arctic Ocean</a>. The border of Asia with Europe is a <a href="/wiki/Social_constructionism" title="Social constructionism">historical and cultural construct</a>, as there is no clear physical and geographical separation between them. A commonly accepted division places Asia to the east of the <a href="/wiki/Suez_Canal" title="Suez Canal">Suez Canal</a> separating it from Africa; and to the east of the <a href="/wiki/Turkish_straits" title="Turkish straits">Turkish straits</a>, the <a href="/wiki/Ural_Mountains" title="Ural Mountains">Ural Mountains</a> and <a href="/wiki/Ural_River" class="mw-redirect" title="Ural River">Ural River</a>, and to the south of the <a href="/wiki/Caucasus_Mountains" title="Caucasus Mountains">Caucasus Mountains</a> and the <a href="/wiki/Caspian_Sea" title="Caspian Sea">Caspian</a> and <a href="/wiki/Black_Sea" title="Black Sea">Black</a> seas, separating it from Europe.<sup id="cite&#95;ref-National&#95;Geographic-1999&#95;15-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-National_Geographic-1999-15"><span class="cite-bracket">&#91;</span>13<span class="cite-bracket">&#93;</span></a></sup>
</p><p>Since the concept of Asia derives from the term for the eastern region from a European perspective, Asia is the remaining vast area of Eurasia minus Europe. Therefore, Asia is a region where various independent cultures coexist rather than sharing a single culture, and its boundary with Europe is somewhat arbitrary and has moved since its first conception in <a href="/wiki/Classical_antiquity" title="Classical antiquity">classical antiquity</a>. The division of Eurasia into two continents reflects <a href="/wiki/East%E2%80%93West_dichotomy" title="East–West dichotomy">East–West</a> cultural differences, some of which vary on a spectrum.
</p><p><a href="/wiki/China" title="China">China</a> and <a href="/wiki/India" title="India">India</a> traded places as the <a href="/wiki/List_of_regions_by_past_GDP_(PPP)" title="List of regions by past GDP (PPP)">largest economies in the world</a> from 1 to 1800 CE. China was a major economic power for much of recorded history, with the highest <a href="/wiki/GDP_per_capita" class="mw-redirect" title="GDP per capita">GDP per capita</a> until 1500.<sup id="cite&#95;ref-16" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-16"><span class="cite-bracket">&#91;</span>14<span class="cite-bracket">&#93;</span></a></sup><sup id="cite&#95;ref-Eric.ed.gov-2000&#95;17-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Eric.ed.gov-2000-17"><span class="cite-bracket">&#91;</span>15<span class="cite-bracket">&#93;</span></a></sup><sup id="cite&#95;ref-18" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-18"><span class="cite-bracket">&#91;</span>16<span class="cite-bracket">&#93;</span></a></sup> The <a href="/wiki/Silk_Road" title="Silk Road">Silk Road</a> became the main east–west trading route in the Asian hinterlands while the <a href="/wiki/Straits_of_Malacca" class="mw-redirect" title="Straits of Malacca">Straits of Malacca</a> stood as a major sea route. Asia has exhibited <a href="/wiki/Economic_dynamism" title="Economic dynamism">economic dynamism</a> as well as robust population growth during the 20th century, but overall population growth has since fallen.<sup id="cite&#95;ref-19" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-19"><span class="cite-bracket">&#91;</span>17<span class="cite-bracket">&#93;</span></a></sup> Asia was the birthplace of most of the world's mainstream religions including <a href="/wiki/Buddhism" title="Buddhism">Buddhism</a>, <a href="/wiki/Christianity" title="Christianity">Christianity</a>, <a href="/wiki/Confucianism" title="Confucianism">Confucianism</a>, <a href="/wiki/Hinduism" title="Hinduism">Hinduism</a>, <a href="/wiki/Islam" title="Islam">Islam</a>, <a href="/wiki/Jainism" title="Jainism">Jainism</a>, <a href="/wiki/Judaism" title="Judaism">Judaism</a>, <a href="/wiki/Sikhism" title="Sikhism">Sikhism</a>, <a href="/wiki/Taoism" title="Taoism">Taoism</a>, <a href="/wiki/Zoroastrianism" title="Zoroastrianism">Zoroastrianism</a>, and many other religions.
</p><p>Asia varies greatly across and within <a href="/wiki/Regions_of_Asia" class="mw-redirect" title="Regions of Asia">its regions</a> with regard to <a href="/wiki/Ethnic_groups" class="mw-redirect" title="Ethnic groups">ethnic groups</a>, cultures, environments, economics, historical ties, and government systems. It also has a mix of many different climates ranging from the equatorial south via the hot deserts in parts of <a href="/wiki/West_Asia" title="West Asia">West Asia</a>, <a href="/wiki/Central_Asia" title="Central Asia">Central Asia</a>, <a href="/wiki/South_Asia" title="South Asia">South Asia</a>, and <a href="/wiki/East_Asia" title="East Asia">East Asia</a> and tropical jungles in <a href="/wiki/Southeast_Asia" title="Southeast Asia">Southeast Asia</a>, temperate areas in the east and the continental centre to vast subarctic and polar areas in <a href="/wiki/North_Asia" title="North Asia">North Asia</a>.
</p>
<meta property="mw:PageProp/toc" />
<div class="mw-heading mw-heading2"><h2 id="Etymology">Etymology</h2></div>
<figure class="mw-default-size mw-halign-left" typeof="mw:File/Thumb"><a href="/wiki/File:Gulf5..JPG" class="mw-file-description"><img src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/f/f6/Gulf5..JPG/250px-Gulf5..JPG" decoding="async" width="250" height="188" class="mw-file-element" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/f/f6/Gulf5..JPG/500px-Gulf5..JPG 1.5x" data-file-width="3072" data-file-height="2304" /></a><figcaption>Ptolemy's Asia</figcaption></figure>
<p>The term "Asia" is believed to originate in the <a href="/wiki/Bronze_Age" title="Bronze Age">Bronze Age</a> toponym <span title="Hittite-language romanization"><i lang="hit-Latn"><a href="/wiki/Assuwa" title="Assuwa">Assuwa</a></i></span> (<a href="/wiki/Hittite_language" title="Hittite language">Hittite</a>: <span lang="hit">𒀸𒋗𒉿</span>, <small>romanized:&#160;</small><span title="Hittite-language romanization"><i lang="hit-Latn">aš-šu-wa</i></span>) which originally referred only to a portion of northwestern <a href="/wiki/Anatolia" title="Anatolia">Anatolia</a>. The term appears in <a href="/wiki/Hittite_texts" class="mw-redirect" title="Hittite texts">Hittite records</a> recounting how a confederation of Assuwan states including <a href="/wiki/Troy" title="Troy">Troy</a> unsuccessfully rebelled against the Hittite king <a href="/wiki/Tudhaliya_I" class="mw-redirect" title="Tudhaliya I">Tudhaliya I</a> around 1400&#160;BCE.<sup id="cite&#95;ref-20" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-20"><span class="cite-bracket">&#91;</span>18<span class="cite-bracket">&#93;</span></a></sup><sup id="cite&#95;ref-21" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-21"><span class="cite-bracket">&#91;</span>19<span class="cite-bracket">&#93;</span></a></sup><sup id="cite&#95;ref-22" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-22"><span class="cite-bracket">&#91;</span>20<span class="cite-bracket">&#93;</span></a></sup> Roughly contemporary <a href="/wiki/Linear_B" title="Linear B">Linear B</a> documents contain the term <span title="Mycenaean Greek-language romanization"><i lang="gmy-Latn">aswia</i></span> (<a href="/wiki/Mycenaean_Greek_language" class="mw-redirect" title="Mycenaean Greek language">Mycenaean Greek</a>: <span lang="gmy">𐀀𐀯𐀹𐀊</span>, <small>romanized:&#160;</small><span title="Mycenaean Greek-language romanization"><i lang="gmy-Latn">a-si-wi-ja</i></span>), seemingly in reference to captives from the same area.<sup id="cite&#95;ref-23" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-23"><span class="cite-bracket">&#91;</span>21<span class="cite-bracket">&#93;</span></a></sup><sup id="cite&#95;ref-24" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-24"><span class="cite-bracket">&#91;</span>22<span class="cite-bracket">&#93;</span></a></sup>
</p>
<figure class="mw-default-size mw-halign-left" typeof="mw:File/Thumb"><a href="/wiki/File:Roman_Empire_-_Asia_(125_AD).svg" class="mw-file-description"><img src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/c/ca/Roman_Empire_-_Asia_%28125_AD%29.svg/250px-Roman_Empire_-_Asia_%28125_AD%29.svg.png" decoding="async" width="250" height="208" class="mw-file-element" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/c/ca/Roman_Empire_-_Asia_%28125_AD%29.svg/500px-Roman_Empire_-_Asia_%28125_AD%29.svg.png 1.5x" data-file-width="2186" data-file-height="1817" /></a><figcaption>The province of <a href="/wiki/Asia_(Roman_province)" title="Asia (Roman province)">Asia</a> within the Roman Empire</figcaption></figure>
<p>Herodotus used the term in reference to <a href="/wiki/Anatolia" title="Anatolia">Anatolia</a> and the territory of the <a href="/wiki/Achaemenid_Empire" title="Achaemenid Empire">Achaemenid Empire</a>, in contrast to Greece and Egypt. He reports that Greeks assumed that Asia was named after the wife of <a href="/wiki/Prometheus" title="Prometheus">Prometheus</a>, but that <a href="/wiki/Lydia" title="Lydia">Lydians</a> say it was named after <i>Asies</i>, son of Cotys, who passed the name on to a tribe at <a href="/wiki/Sardis" title="Sardis">Sardis</a>.<sup id="cite&#95;ref-25" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-25"><span class="cite-bracket">&#91;</span>23<span class="cite-bracket">&#93;</span></a></sup> In <a href="/wiki/Greek_mythology" title="Greek mythology">Greek mythology</a>, "Asia" (<span title="Ancient Greek (to 1453)-language text"><span lang="grc">Ἀσία</span></span> or <span title="Ancient Greek (to 1453)-language text"><span lang="grc">Ἀσίη</span></span>) was the name of a "<a href="/wiki/Nymph" title="Nymph">Nymph</a> or <a href="/wiki/Titan_(mythology)" class="mw-redirect" title="Titan (mythology)">Titan</a> goddess of Lydia".<sup id="cite&#95;ref-26" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-26"><span class="cite-bracket">&#91;</span>24<span class="cite-bracket">&#93;</span></a></sup> The <a href="/wiki/Iliad" title="Iliad">Iliad</a> (attributed by the ancient Greeks to <a href="/wiki/Homer" title="Homer">Homer</a>) mentions two Phrygians in the <a href="/wiki/Trojan_War" title="Trojan War">Trojan War</a> named <a href="/wiki/Asius_(mythology)" title="Asius (mythology)">Asios</a> (literally 'Asian');<sup id="cite&#95;ref-27" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-27"><span class="cite-bracket">&#91;</span>25<span class="cite-bracket">&#93;</span></a></sup> and also a marsh or lowland containing a marsh in Lydia as <span title="Ancient Greek (to 1453)-language text"><span lang="grc">ασιος</span></span>.<sup id="cite&#95;ref-28" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-28"><span class="cite-bracket">&#91;</span>26<span class="cite-bracket">&#93;</span></a></sup>
</p><p>The term was later adopted by the <a href="/wiki/Roman_people" title="Roman people">Romans</a>, who used it in reference to the <a href="/wiki/Province_of_Asia" class="mw-redirect" title="Province of Asia">province of Asia</a>, located in western Anatolia.<sup id="cite&#95;ref-29" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-29"><span class="cite-bracket">&#91;</span>27<span class="cite-bracket">&#93;</span></a></sup> One of the first writers to use Asia as a name of the whole continent was <a href="/wiki/Pliny_the_Elder" title="Pliny the Elder">Pliny</a>.<sup id="cite&#95;ref-30" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-30"><span class="cite-bracket">&#91;</span>28<span class="cite-bracket">&#93;</span></a></sup>
</p>
<div style="clear:both;" class=""></div>
<div class="mw-heading mw-heading2"><h2 id="Definition">Definition</h2></div>
<link rel="mw-deduplicated-inline-style" href="mw-data:TemplateStyles:r1320445320" /><div role="note" class="hatnote navigation-not-searchable">Further information&#32;on Asian borders: <a href="/wiki/Geography_of_Asia#Boundary" title="Geography of Asia">Geography of Asia §&#160;Boundary</a>, <a href="/wiki/Boundaries_between_the_continents" title="Boundaries between the continents">Boundaries between the continents</a>, <a href="/wiki/List_of_transcontinental_countries#Asia_and_Europe" title="List of transcontinental countries">List of transcontinental countries §&#160;Asia and Europe</a>, and <a href="/wiki/Copenhagen_criteria" title="Copenhagen criteria">Copenhagen criteria</a></div>
<div class="mw-heading mw-heading3"><h3 id="Asia–Europe_boundary"><span id="Asia.E2.80.93Europe_boundary"></span>Asia–Europe boundary</h3></div>
<figure class="mw-default-size" typeof="mw:File/Thumb"><a href="/wiki/File:Le_varie_convenzioni_sul_confine_Europa-Asia.png" class="mw-file-description"><img src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/3/3e/Le_varie_convenzioni_sul_confine_Europa-Asia.png/330px-Le_varie_convenzioni_sul_confine_Europa-Asia.png" decoding="async" width="300" height="465" class="mw-file-element" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/3/3e/Le_varie_convenzioni_sul_confine_Europa-Asia.png/500px-Le_varie_convenzioni_sul_confine_Europa-Asia.png 1.5x, //upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/3/3e/Le_varie_convenzioni_sul_confine_Europa-Asia.png 2x" data-file-width="729" data-file-height="1129" /></a><figcaption>Definitions used for the boundary between Asia and Europe: <br /><b>A</b> <a href="/wiki/Ural_Mountains" title="Ural Mountains">Ural Mountains</a> - <a href="/wiki/Emba_(river)" title="Emba (river)">Emba river</a> - <a href="/wiki/Kuma%E2%80%93Manych_Depression" title="Kuma–Manych Depression">Kuma–Manych Depression</a> <br /><b>B</b> <a href="/wiki/Ural_Mountains" title="Ural Mountains">Ural Mountains</a> - <a href="/wiki/Ural_(river)" title="Ural (river)">Ural river</a> - <a href="/wiki/Kuma%E2%80%93Manych_Depression" title="Kuma–Manych Depression">Kuma–Manych Depression</a><br /><b>C</b> <a href="/wiki/Ural_Mountains" title="Ural Mountains">Ural Mountains</a> - <a href="/wiki/Ural_(river)" title="Ural (river)">Ural river</a> - <a href="/wiki/Caucasus_Mountains" title="Caucasus Mountains">Caucasus Mountains</a>.</figcaption></figure>
<p>The threefold division of the <a href="/wiki/Old_World" title="Old World">Old World</a> into Africa, Asia, and Europe has been in use since the 6th century&#160;BCE, due to <a href="/wiki/Greek_geographers" class="mw-redirect" title="Greek geographers">Greek geographers</a> such as <a href="/wiki/Anaximander" title="Anaximander">Anaximander</a> and <a href="/wiki/Hecataeus_of_Miletus" title="Hecataeus of Miletus">Hecataeus</a>.<sup id="cite&#95;ref-31" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-31"><span class="cite-bracket">&#91;</span>29<span class="cite-bracket">&#93;</span></a></sup> Anaximander placed the boundary between Asia and Europe along the <a href="/wiki/Phasis_River" class="mw-redirect" title="Phasis River">Phasis River</a> (now the Rioni) in <a href="/wiki/Georgia_(country)" title="Georgia (country)">Georgia</a> of Caucasus (from its mouth by <a href="/wiki/Poti" title="Poti">Poti</a> on the <a href="/wiki/Black_Sea" title="Black Sea">Black Sea</a> coast, through the <a href="/wiki/Surami_Pass" title="Surami Pass">Surami Pass</a> and along the <a href="/wiki/Kura_(Caspian_Sea)" class="mw-redirect" title="Kura (Caspian Sea)">Kura River</a> to the Caspian Sea), a convention still followed by <a href="/wiki/Herodotus" title="Herodotus">Herodotus</a> in the 5th century&#160;BCE.<sup id="cite&#95;ref-32" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-32"><span class="cite-bracket">&#91;</span>30<span class="cite-bracket">&#93;</span></a></sup> During the <a href="/wiki/Hellenistic_period" title="Hellenistic period">Hellenistic period</a>,<sup id="cite&#95;ref-33" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-33"><span class="cite-bracket">&#91;</span>31<span class="cite-bracket">&#93;</span></a></sup> this convention was revised, and the boundary between Europe and Asia was now considered to be the <a href="/wiki/Don_River_(Russia)" class="mw-redirect" title="Don River (Russia)">Tanais</a> (the modern Don River). This is the convention used by Roman era authors such as <a href="/wiki/Posidonius" title="Posidonius">Posidonius</a>,<sup id="cite&#95;ref-34" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-34"><span class="cite-bracket">&#91;</span>32<span class="cite-bracket">&#93;</span></a></sup> <a href="/wiki/Strabo" title="Strabo">Strabo</a><sup id="cite&#95;ref-35" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-35"><span class="cite-bracket">&#91;</span>33<span class="cite-bracket">&#93;</span></a></sup> and <a href="/wiki/Ptolemy" title="Ptolemy">Ptolemy</a>.<sup id="cite&#95;ref-36" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-36"><span class="cite-bracket">&#91;</span>34<span class="cite-bracket">&#93;</span></a></sup>
</p><p>The border between Asia and Europe was historically defined by European academics.<sup id="cite&#95;ref-Lineback-2013&#95;37-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Lineback-2013-37"><span class="cite-bracket">&#91;</span>35<span class="cite-bracket">&#93;</span></a></sup>
</p><p>In Sweden, five years after Peter's death, in 1730 <a href="/wiki/Philip_Johan_von_Strahlenberg" title="Philip Johan von Strahlenberg">Philip Johan von Strahlenberg</a> published a new atlas proposing the Ural Mountains as the border of Asia. Tatishchev announced that he had proposed the idea to von Strahlenberg. The latter had suggested the <a href="/wiki/Emba_River" class="mw-redirect" title="Emba River">Emba River</a> as the lower boundary. Over the next century various proposals were made until the <a href="/wiki/Ural_River" class="mw-redirect" title="Ural River">Ural River</a> prevailed in the mid-19th century. The border had been moved perforce from the Black Sea to the Caspian Sea into which the Ural River projects.<sup id="cite&#95;ref-38" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-38"><span class="cite-bracket">&#91;</span>36<span class="cite-bracket">&#93;</span></a></sup> The border between the Black Sea and the Caspian is often placed along the crest of the <a href="/wiki/Caucasus_Mountains" title="Caucasus Mountains">Caucasus Mountains</a>, although it is sometimes placed further north,<sup id="cite&#95;ref-Lineback-2013&#95;37-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Lineback-2013-37"><span class="cite-bracket">&#91;</span>35<span class="cite-bracket">&#93;</span></a></sup> on the <a href="/wiki/Kuma%E2%80%93Manych_Depression" title="Kuma–Manych Depression">Kuma–Manych Depression</a>.
</p>
<div class="mw-heading mw-heading3"><h3 id="Asia–Africa_boundary"><span id="Asia.E2.80.93Africa_boundary"></span>Asia–Africa boundary</h3></div>
<p>The boundary between Asia and Africa is the <a href="/wiki/Suez_Canal" title="Suez Canal">Suez Canal</a>, the <a href="/wiki/Gulf_of_Suez" title="Gulf of Suez">Gulf of Suez</a>, the <a href="/wiki/Red_Sea" title="Red Sea">Red Sea</a>, and the <a href="/wiki/Bab-el-Mandeb" title="Bab-el-Mandeb">Bab-el-Mandeb</a>.<sup id="cite&#95;ref-39" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-39"><span class="cite-bracket">&#91;</span>37<span class="cite-bracket">&#93;</span></a></sup> This makes <a href="/wiki/Egypt" title="Egypt">Egypt</a> a <a href="/wiki/Transcontinental_country" class="mw-redirect" title="Transcontinental country">transcontinental country</a>, with the <a href="/wiki/Sinai_Peninsula" title="Sinai Peninsula">Sinai Peninsula</a> in Asia and the remainder of the country in Africa.
</p>
<div class="mw-heading mw-heading3"><h3 id="Asia–Oceania_boundary"><span id="Asia.E2.80.93Oceania_boundary"></span>Asia–Oceania boundary</h3></div>
<figure class="mw-default-size mw-halign-left" typeof="mw:File/Thumb"><a href="/wiki/File:Map_of_Sunda_and_Sahul.svg" class="mw-file-description"><img src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/6/6d/Map_of_Sunda_and_Sahul.svg/330px-Map_of_Sunda_and_Sahul.svg.png" decoding="async" width="300" height="279" class="mw-file-element" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/6/6d/Map_of_Sunda_and_Sahul.svg/500px-Map_of_Sunda_and_Sahul.svg.png 1.5x, //upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/6/6d/Map_of_Sunda_and_Sahul.svg/960px-Map_of_Sunda_and_Sahul.svg.png 2x" data-file-width="976" data-file-height="907" /></a><figcaption>Definitions of the boundary between Asia and Oceania</figcaption></figure>
<p>The border between Asia and <a href="/wiki/Oceania" title="Oceania">Oceania</a> is usually placed somewhere in the <a href="/wiki/Indonesian_Archipelago" class="mw-redirect" title="Indonesian Archipelago">Indonesian Archipelago</a>, specifically in <a href="/wiki/Eastern_Indonesia" title="Eastern Indonesia">Eastern Indonesia</a>. The <a href="/wiki/Wallace_Line" title="Wallace Line">Wallace Line</a> separates the Asian and <a href="/wiki/Wallacea" title="Wallacea">Wallacea</a> biogeographical realms, a transition zone of deep water straits between the Asian and Australian continental shelves. <a href="/wiki/Weber%27s_Line" class="mw-redirect" title="Weber&#39;s Line">Weber's Line</a> split the region in two with regard to the balance of fauna between Asian origin or Australo-Papuan origin.<sup id="cite&#95;ref-Simpson-1977&#95;40-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Simpson-1977-40"><span class="cite-bracket">&#91;</span>38<span class="cite-bracket">&#93;</span></a></sup> Wallacea's eastern boundary with <a href="/wiki/Australia_(continent)" title="Australia (continent)">Sahul</a> is represented by the <a href="/wiki/Richard_Lydekker" title="Richard Lydekker">Lydekker's Line</a>.<sup id="cite&#95;ref-41" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-41"><span class="cite-bracket">&#91;</span>39<span class="cite-bracket">&#93;</span></a></sup><sup id="cite&#95;ref-42" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-42"><span class="cite-bracket">&#91;</span>40<span class="cite-bracket">&#93;</span></a></sup> The <a href="/wiki/Maluku_Islands" title="Maluku Islands">Maluku Islands</a> (except the <a href="/wiki/Aru_Islands_Regency" title="Aru Islands Regency">Aru Islands</a>) are often considered to lie on the border of southeast Asia, with the Aru Islands and <a href="/wiki/Western_New_Guinea" title="Western New Guinea">Western New Guinea</a>, to the east of the Lydekker's Line, being wholly part of Oceania, as both lie on the Australian continental plate.<sup id="cite&#95;ref-Simpson-1977&#95;40-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Simpson-1977-40"><span class="cite-bracket">&#91;</span>38<span class="cite-bracket">&#93;</span></a></sup> Culturally, the Wallacea region denoted the transition between <a href="/wiki/Austronesian_people" class="mw-redirect" title="Austronesian people">Austronesian</a> and <a href="/wiki/Melanesian_people" class="mw-redirect" title="Melanesian people">Melanesian people</a>, with varying degrees of intermixing between the two. In general, the further west and coastal a region is, the stronger the Austronesian influences, and the further east and inland a region is, the stronger the Melanesian influences.<sup id="cite&#95;ref-43" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-43"><span class="cite-bracket">&#91;</span>41<span class="cite-bracket">&#93;</span></a></sup> The terms Southeast Asia and Oceania, devised in the 19th century, have had several vastly different geographic meanings since their inception. The chief factor in determining which islands of the Indonesian Archipelago are Asian has been the location of the colonial possessions of the various empires there (not all European). Lewis and Wigen assert, "The narrowing of 'Southeast Asia' to its present boundaries was thus a gradual process."<sup id="cite&#95;ref-44" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-44"><span class="cite-bracket">&#91;</span>42<span class="cite-bracket">&#93;</span></a></sup>
</p>
<div class="mw-heading mw-heading3"><h3 id="Asia–North_America_boundary"><span id="Asia.E2.80.93North_America_boundary"></span>Asia–North America boundary</h3></div>
<link rel="mw-deduplicated-inline-style" href="mw-data:TemplateStyles:r1320445320" /><div role="note" class="hatnote navigation-not-searchable">See also: <a href="/wiki/Northeast_Asia" title="Northeast Asia">Northeast Asia</a></div>
<figure class="mw-default-size" typeof="mw:File/Thumb"><a href="/wiki/File:Us-su-maritime.jpg" class="mw-file-description"><img src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/9/98/Us-su-maritime.jpg/330px-Us-su-maritime.jpg" decoding="async" width="300" height="261" class="mw-file-element" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/9/98/Us-su-maritime.jpg/500px-Us-su-maritime.jpg 1.5x, //upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/9/98/Us-su-maritime.jpg 2x" data-file-width="600" data-file-height="522" /></a><figcaption>The United States–Russia border according to the <a href="/wiki/USSR%E2%80%93USA_Maritime_Boundary_Agreement" title="USSR–USA Maritime Boundary Agreement">USSR–USA Maritime Boundary Agreement</a></figcaption></figure>
<p>The <a href="/wiki/Bering_Strait" title="Bering Strait">Bering Strait</a> and <a href="/wiki/Bering_Sea" title="Bering Sea">Bering Sea</a> separate the landmasses of Asia and <a href="/wiki/North_America" title="North America">North America</a>, as well as forming the international boundary between Russia and the United States. This <a href="/wiki/National_boundary" class="mw-redirect" title="National boundary">national</a> and continental boundary separates the <a href="/wiki/Diomede_Islands" title="Diomede Islands">Diomede Islands</a> in the Bering Strait, with <a href="/wiki/Diomede_Islands" title="Diomede Islands">Big Diomede</a> in <a href="/wiki/Russia" title="Russia">Russia</a> and <a href="/wiki/Diomede_Islands" title="Diomede Islands">Little Diomede</a> in the <a href="/wiki/United_States" title="United States">United States</a>. The <a href="/wiki/Aleutian_Islands" title="Aleutian Islands">Aleutian Islands</a> are an island chain extending westward from the <a href="/wiki/Alaskan_Peninsula" class="mw-redirect" title="Alaskan Peninsula">Alaskan Peninsula</a> toward Russia's <a href="/wiki/Komandorski_Islands" class="mw-redirect" title="Komandorski Islands">Komandorski Islands</a> and <a href="/wiki/Kamchatka_Peninsula" title="Kamchatka Peninsula">Kamchatka Peninsula</a>. Most of them are always associated with North America, except for the westernmost <a href="/wiki/Near_Islands" title="Near Islands">Near Islands</a> group, which is on Asia's continental shelf beyond the <a href="/wiki/North_Aleutians_Basin" title="North Aleutians Basin">North Aleutians Basin</a> and on rare occasions could be associated with Asia, which could then allow the United States to be considered a transcontinental state. The Aleutian Islands are sometimes associated with Oceania, owing to their status as remote Pacific islands, and their proximity to the Pacific Plate.<sup id="cite&#95;ref-45" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-45"><span class="cite-bracket">&#91;</span>43<span class="cite-bracket">&#93;</span></a></sup><sup id="cite&#95;ref-46" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-46"><span class="cite-bracket">&#91;</span>44<span class="cite-bracket">&#93;</span></a></sup><sup id="cite&#95;ref-47" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-47"><span class="cite-bracket">&#91;</span>45<span class="cite-bracket">&#93;</span></a></sup> This is extremely rare however, due to their non-tropical biogeography, as well as their <a href="/wiki/Aleut" class="mw-redirect" title="Aleut">inhabitants</a>, who have historically been related to the <a href="/wiki/Indigenous_peoples_of_the_Americas" title="Indigenous peoples of the Americas">Indigenous peoples of the Americas</a>.<sup id="cite&#95;ref-48" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-48"><span class="cite-bracket">&#91;</span>46<span class="cite-bracket">&#93;</span></a></sup><sup id="cite&#95;ref-49" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-49"><span class="cite-bracket">&#91;</span>47<span class="cite-bracket">&#93;</span></a></sup>
</p><p><a href="/wiki/St._Lawrence_Island" title="St. Lawrence Island">St. Lawrence Island</a> in the northern Bering Sea belongs to the US state of <a href="/wiki/Alaska" title="Alaska">Alaska</a> and may be associated with either continent but is almost always considered part of North America, as with the <a href="/wiki/Rat_Islands" title="Rat Islands">Rat Islands</a> in the Aleutian chain. At their nearest points, Alaska and Russia are separated by only 4 kilometres (2.5 miles).
</p>
<div class="mw-heading mw-heading3"><h3 id="Ongoing_definition">Ongoing definition</h3></div>
<figure class="mw-default-size mw-halign-left" typeof="mw:File/Thumb"><a href="/wiki/File:Afro-Eurasia_(orthographic_projection)_political.svg" class="mw-file-description"><img src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/9/9d/Afro-Eurasia_%28orthographic_projection%29_political.svg/250px-Afro-Eurasia_%28orthographic_projection%29_political.svg.png" decoding="async" width="250" height="250" class="mw-file-element" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/9/9d/Afro-Eurasia_%28orthographic_projection%29_political.svg/500px-Afro-Eurasia_%28orthographic_projection%29_political.svg.png 1.5x" data-file-width="550" data-file-height="550" /></a><figcaption>Afro-Eurasia</figcaption></figure>
<p>Geographical Asia is a cultural artifact of European conceptions of the world, beginning with the <a href="/wiki/Ancient_Greeks" class="mw-redirect" title="Ancient Greeks">Ancient Greeks</a>, being imposed onto other cultures, an imprecise concept causing endemic contention about what it means. Asia does not exactly correspond to the cultural borders of its various types of constituents.<sup id="cite&#95;ref-50" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-50"><span class="cite-bracket">&#91;</span>48<span class="cite-bracket">&#93;</span></a></sup>
</p><p>From the time of Herodotus, a minority of geographers have rejected the three-continent system (Europe, Africa, Asia) on the grounds that there is no substantial physical separation between them.<sup id="cite&#95;ref-51" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-51"><span class="cite-bracket">&#91;</span>49<span class="cite-bracket">&#93;</span></a></sup> For example, Sir <a href="/wiki/Barry_Cunliffe" title="Barry Cunliffe">Barry Cunliffe</a>, the emeritus professor of European archeology at Oxford, argues that Europe has been geographically and culturally merely "the western excrescence of the continent of Asia".<sup id="cite&#95;ref-52" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-52"><span class="cite-bracket">&#91;</span>50<span class="cite-bracket">&#93;</span></a></sup>
</p><p>Geographically, Asia is the major eastern constituent of the continent of <a href="/wiki/Eurasia" title="Eurasia">Eurasia</a> with Europe being a northwestern <a href="/wiki/Peninsula" title="Peninsula">peninsula</a> of the landmass. Asia, Europe and Africa make up a single continuous landmass—<a href="/wiki/Afro-Eurasia" title="Afro-Eurasia">Afro-Eurasia</a>—and share a common <a href="/wiki/Continental_shelf" title="Continental shelf">continental shelf</a>. Almost all of Europe and a major part of Asia sit atop the <a href="/wiki/Eurasian_Plate" class="mw-redirect" title="Eurasian Plate">Eurasian Plate</a>, adjoined on the south by the <a href="/wiki/Arabian_Plate" class="mw-redirect" title="Arabian Plate">Arabian</a> and <a href="/wiki/Indian_Plate" class="mw-redirect" title="Indian Plate">Indian Plate</a> and with the easternmost part of Siberia (east of the <a href="/wiki/Chersky_Range" title="Chersky Range">Chersky Range</a>) on the <a href="/wiki/North_American_Plate" class="mw-redirect" title="North American Plate">North American Plate</a>.
</p>
<div style="clear:both;" class=""></div>
<div class="mw-heading mw-heading2"><h2 id="History">History</h2></div>
<link rel="mw-deduplicated-inline-style" href="mw-data:TemplateStyles:r1320445320" /><div role="note" class="hatnote navigation-not-searchable">Main article: <a href="/wiki/History_of_Asia" title="History of Asia">History of Asia</a></div>
<div class="mw-heading mw-heading3"><h3 id="Prehistory">Prehistory</h3></div>
<link rel="mw-deduplicated-inline-style" href="mw-data:TemplateStyles:r1320445320" /><div role="note" class="hatnote navigation-not-searchable">See also: <a href="/wiki/Prehistoric_Asia" title="Prehistoric Asia">Prehistoric Asia</a></div>
<figure class="mw-default-size" typeof="mw:File/Thumb"><a href="/wiki/File:Spreading_homo_sapiens_la.svg" class="mw-file-description"><img src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/2/27/Spreading_homo_sapiens_la.svg/250px-Spreading_homo_sapiens_la.svg.png" decoding="async" width="250" height="117" class="mw-file-element" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/2/27/Spreading_homo_sapiens_la.svg/500px-Spreading_homo_sapiens_la.svg.png 1.5x" data-file-width="1692" data-file-height="791" /></a><figcaption>Map of <a href="/wiki/Early_human_migrations" title="Early human migrations">early human migrations</a> based on the <a href="/wiki/Recent_African_origin_of_modern_humans" title="Recent African origin of modern humans">Out of Africa theory</a>.</figcaption></figure>
<figure class="mw-default-size" typeof="mw:File/Thumb"><a href="/wiki/File:Indo-European_migrations_and_Ancient_Northeast_Asians.png" class="mw-file-description"><img src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/6/6a/Indo-European_migrations_and_Ancient_Northeast_Asians.png/250px-Indo-European_migrations_and_Ancient_Northeast_Asians.png" decoding="async" width="250" height="103" class="mw-file-element" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/6/6a/Indo-European_migrations_and_Ancient_Northeast_Asians.png/500px-Indo-European_migrations_and_Ancient_Northeast_Asians.png 1.5x" data-file-width="6652" data-file-height="2744" /></a><figcaption>Early <a href="/wiki/Indo-European_migrations" title="Indo-European migrations">Indo-European migrations</a> from the Pontic steppes and across Central Asia, and encounter with <a href="/wiki/Ancient_Northeast_Asian" title="Ancient Northeast Asian">Ancient Northeast Asian</a> populations.</figcaption></figure>
<p>About 1.8 million years ago, <i><a href="/wiki/Homo_erectus" title="Homo erectus">Homo erectus</a></i> left the African continent.<sup id="cite&#95;ref-53" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-53"><span class="cite-bracket">&#91;</span>51<span class="cite-bracket">&#93;</span></a></sup> This species is believed to have lived in East and Southeast Asia from 1.8 million to 110,000 years ago.<sup id="cite&#95;ref-54" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-54"><span class="cite-bracket">&#91;</span>52<span class="cite-bracket">&#93;</span></a></sup>
</p><p>Researchers believe that the modern human, or <i><a href="/wiki/Homo_sapiens" class="mw-redirect" title="Homo sapiens">Homo sapiens</a></i>, migrated about 60,000 years ago to <a href="/wiki/South_Asia" title="South Asia">South Asia</a> along the Indian Ocean.<sup id="cite&#95;ref-55" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-55"><span class="cite-bracket">&#91;</span>53<span class="cite-bracket">&#93;</span></a></sup> Modern humans <a href="/wiki/Interbreeding_between_archaic_and_modern_humans" title="Interbreeding between archaic and modern humans">interbred</a> with an <a href="/wiki/Archaic_human" class="mw-redirect" title="Archaic human">archaic human</a> species called <a href="/wiki/Denisovans" class="mw-redirect" title="Denisovans">Denisovans</a> in Southeast Asia.<sup id="cite&#95;ref-56" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-56"><span class="cite-bracket">&#91;</span>54<span class="cite-bracket">&#93;</span></a></sup> Before the arrival of modern humans, <a href="/wiki/Flores" title="Flores">Flores</a> was occupied by <i><a href="/wiki/Homo_floresiensis" title="Homo floresiensis">Homo floresiensis</a></i>, a small archaic human.<sup id="cite&#95;ref-57" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-57"><span class="cite-bracket">&#91;</span>55<span class="cite-bracket">&#93;</span></a></sup>
</p><p>Ancestors of <a href="/wiki/Ancient_East_Eurasians" title="Ancient East Eurasians">East Eurasians</a> split from Ancient West Eurasians about 46,000 years ago, migrating out of the hub in the <a href="/wiki/Iranian_Plateau" class="mw-redirect" title="Iranian Plateau">Iranian Plateau</a>.<sup id="cite&#95;ref-:0&#95;58-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-:0-58"><span class="cite-bracket">&#91;</span>56<span class="cite-bracket">&#93;</span></a></sup> Evidence from full genomic studies suggests that the first people in the Americas diverged from <a href="/wiki/Genetic_history_of_East_Asians" title="Genetic history of East Asians">Ancient East Asians</a> about 36,000 years ago and expanded northwards into Siberia, where they encountered and interacted with a different Paleolithic Siberian population (known as <a href="/wiki/Ancient_North_Eurasian" title="Ancient North Eurasian">Ancient North Eurasians</a>), giving rise to both <a href="/wiki/Paleosiberian_peoples" class="mw-redirect" title="Paleosiberian peoples">Paleosiberian peoples</a> and <a href="/wiki/Ancient_Beringian" title="Ancient Beringian">Ancient Native Americans</a>.<sup id="cite&#95;ref-59" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-59"><span class="cite-bracket">&#91;</span>57<span class="cite-bracket">&#93;</span></a></sup>
</p><p>Modern <a href="/wiki/Genetics_and_archaeogenetics_of_South_Asia" title="Genetics and archaeogenetics of South Asia">South Asians</a> are descendants of a combination of Western Eurasian ancestries (notably <a href="/wiki/Iranian_hunter-gatherers" title="Iranian hunter-gatherers">Neolithic Iranian</a> and <a href="/wiki/Western_Steppe_Herders" title="Western Steppe Herders">Western Steppe Herder</a> components) with a proposed indigenous <a href="/wiki/Ancient_East_Eurasians" title="Ancient East Eurasians">East Eurasian</a> component (termed <i>Ancient Ancestral South Indians</i>) closest to the non–West Eurasian part extracted from South Asian samples; distantly related to the <a href="/wiki/Andamanese_people" class="mw-redirect" title="Andamanese people">Andamanese peoples</a>, East Asians and <a href="/wiki/Aboriginal_Australians" title="Aboriginal Australians">Aboriginal Australians</a>.<sup id="cite&#95;ref-Narasimhan&#95;2019&#95;60-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Narasimhan_2019-60"><span class="cite-bracket">&#91;</span>58<span class="cite-bracket">&#93;</span></a></sup>
</p>
<div class="mw-heading mw-heading3"><h3 id="Ancient_era">Ancient era</h3></div>
<link rel="mw-deduplicated-inline-style" href="mw-data:TemplateStyles:r1320445320" /><div role="note" class="hatnote navigation-not-searchable">See also: <a href="/wiki/Sinosphere" title="Sinosphere">Sinosphere</a>, <a href="/wiki/Greater_India" title="Greater India">Greater India</a>, and <a href="/wiki/Greater_Iran" title="Greater Iran">Greater Iran</a></div><figure class="mw-default-size" typeof="mw:File/Thumb"><a href="/wiki/File:Silkroutes.jpg" class="mw-file-description"><img src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/c/cb/Silkroutes.jpg/330px-Silkroutes.jpg" decoding="async" width="300" height="264" class="mw-file-element" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/c/cb/Silkroutes.jpg/500px-Silkroutes.jpg 1.5x, //upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/c/cb/Silkroutes.jpg 2x" data-file-width="750" data-file-height="660" /></a><figcaption>The <a href="/wiki/Silk_Road" title="Silk Road">Silk Road</a> connected civilisations across Asia.<sup id="cite&#95;ref-61" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-61"><span class="cite-bracket">&#91;</span>59<span class="cite-bracket">&#93;</span></a></sup></figcaption></figure><p>The history of Asia can be seen as the distinct histories of several peripheral coastal regions: <a href="/wiki/East_Asia" title="East Asia">East Asia</a>, <a href="/wiki/South_Asia" title="South Asia">South Asia</a>, <a href="/wiki/Southeast_Asia" title="Southeast Asia">Southeast Asia</a>, <a href="/wiki/Central_Asia" title="Central Asia">Central Asia</a>, and <a href="/wiki/West_Asia" title="West Asia">West Asia</a>. The coastal periphery was home to some of the world's earliest known civilisations, each of them developing around fertile river valleys. The civilisations in <a href="/wiki/Mesopotamia" title="Mesopotamia">Mesopotamia</a>, the <a href="/wiki/Indus_Valley" class="mw-redirect" title="Indus Valley">Indus Valley</a> and the <a href="/wiki/Yellow_River" title="Yellow River">Yellow River</a> shared many similarities. These civilisations may well have exchanged technologies and ideas such as <a href="/wiki/Mathematics" title="Mathematics">mathematics</a> and the <a href="/wiki/Wheel" title="Wheel">wheel</a>. Other innovations, such as writing, seem to have been developed individually in each area. Cities, states and empires developed in these lowlands.
</p><p>The central steppe region had long been inhabited by horse-mounted nomads who could reach all areas of Asia from the <a href="/wiki/Steppe" title="Steppe">steppes</a>. The earliest postulated <a href="/wiki/Indo-European_migrations" title="Indo-European migrations">expansion</a> out of the steppe is that of the <a href="/wiki/Indo-Europeans" class="mw-redirect" title="Indo-Europeans">Indo-Europeans</a>, who spread their languages into West Asia, South Asia, and the borders of China,<sup id="cite&#95;ref-Narasimhan&#95;2019&#95;60-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Narasimhan_2019-60"><span class="cite-bracket">&#91;</span>58<span class="cite-bracket">&#93;</span></a></sup> where the <a href="/wiki/Tocharians" title="Tocharians">Tocharians</a> resided. The northernmost part of Asia, including much of <a href="/wiki/Siberia" title="Siberia">Siberia</a>, was largely inaccessible to the steppe nomads, owing to the dense forests, climate and <a href="/wiki/Tundra" title="Tundra">tundra</a>. These areas remained very sparsely populated.
</p><p>The center and the peripheries were mostly kept separated by mountains and deserts. The <a href="/wiki/Caucasus" title="Caucasus">Caucasus</a> and <a href="/wiki/Himalaya" class="mw-redirect" title="Himalaya">Himalaya</a> mountains and the <a href="/wiki/Karakum_Desert" title="Karakum Desert">Karakum</a> and <a href="/wiki/Gobi" class="mw-redirect" title="Gobi">Gobi</a> deserts formed barriers that the <a href="/wiki/Eurasian_nomads" title="Eurasian nomads">steppe horsemen</a> could cross only with difficulty. While the urban city dwellers were more advanced technologically and socially, in many cases they could do little in a military aspect to defend against the <a href="/wiki/Nomadic_empire" title="Nomadic empire">mounted hordes</a> of the steppe. However, the lowlands did not have enough open grasslands to support a large equestrian force; for this and other reasons, the nomads who conquered states in China, India, and the Middle East often found themselves adapting to the local, more affluent societies.
</p>
<div class="mw-heading mw-heading3"><h3 id="Medieval_era">Medieval era</h3></div>
<figure class="mw-default-size" typeof="mw:File/Thumb"><a href="/wiki/File:Mongol_dominions1.jpg" class="mw-file-description"><img src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/3/3f/Mongol_dominions1.jpg/330px-Mongol_dominions1.jpg" decoding="async" width="300" height="220" class="mw-file-element" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/3/3f/Mongol_dominions1.jpg/500px-Mongol_dominions1.jpg 1.5x, //upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/3/3f/Mongol_dominions1.jpg/960px-Mongol_dominions1.jpg 2x" data-file-width="1280" data-file-height="938" /></a><figcaption>The <a href="/wiki/Mongol_Empire" title="Mongol Empire">Mongol Empire</a> at its greatest extent. The gray area is the later <a href="/wiki/Timurid_Empire" title="Timurid Empire">Timurid Empire</a>.</figcaption></figure><p>The Islamic <a href="/wiki/Caliphate" title="Caliphate">Caliphate</a>'s defeats of the <a href="/wiki/Byzantine" class="mw-redirect" title="Byzantine">Byzantine</a> and Persian empires led to West Asia and southern parts of <a href="/wiki/Central_Asia" title="Central Asia">Central Asia</a> and <a href="/wiki/Northwestern_South_Asia" title="Northwestern South Asia">western parts of South Asia</a> under its control during <a href="/wiki/Early_Muslim_conquests" title="Early Muslim conquests">its conquests</a> of the 7th century; Islam also <a href="/wiki/Spread_of_Islam" title="Spread of Islam">spread</a> over centuries to the southern regions of India and Southeast Asia through trade along the <a href="/wiki/Maritime_Silk_Road" title="Maritime Silk Road">Maritime Silk Road</a>.<sup id="cite&#95;ref-62" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-62"><span class="cite-bracket">&#91;</span>60<span class="cite-bracket">&#93;</span></a></sup><sup id="cite&#95;ref-63" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-63"><span class="cite-bracket">&#91;</span>61<span class="cite-bracket">&#93;</span></a></sup> The <a href="/wiki/Mongol_Empire" title="Mongol Empire">Mongol Empire</a> conquered a large part of Asia in the 13th century, an area extending from China to Europe. Before the Mongol invasion, <a href="/wiki/Song_dynasty" title="Song dynasty">Song dynasty</a> reportedly had approximately 120 million citizens; the 1300 census which followed the invasion reported roughly 60 million people.<sup id="cite&#95;ref-64" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-64"><span class="cite-bracket">&#91;</span>62<span class="cite-bracket">&#93;</span></a></sup>
</p><p>The <a href="/wiki/Black_Death" title="Black Death">Black Death</a>, one of the most devastating <a href="/wiki/Pandemic" title="Pandemic">pandemics</a> in human history, is thought to have originated in the arid plains of central Asia, where it then travelled along the <a href="/wiki/Silk_Road" title="Silk Road">Silk Road</a>.<sup id="cite&#95;ref-65" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-65"><span class="cite-bracket">&#91;</span>63<span class="cite-bracket">&#93;</span></a></sup>
</p>
<div class="mw-heading mw-heading3"><h3 id="Modern_era">Modern era</h3></div>
<link rel="mw-deduplicated-inline-style" href="mw-data:TemplateStyles:r1320445320" /><div role="note" class="hatnote navigation-not-searchable">See also: <a href="/wiki/History_of_Eurasia#Modern_era" title="History of Eurasia">History of Eurasia §&#160;Modern era</a>, and <a href="/wiki/Afro-Asia#Modern_era" title="Afro-Asia">Afro-Asia §&#160;Modern era</a></div>
<p>European involvement in Asia became more significant from the <a href="/wiki/Age_of_Discovery" title="Age of Discovery">Age of Discovery</a> onward, with Iberian-sponsored sailors such as <a href="/wiki/Christopher_Columbus" title="Christopher Columbus">Christopher Columbus</a> and <a href="/wiki/Vasco_da_Gama" title="Vasco da Gama">Vasco da Gama</a> paving the way for new routes from <a href="/wiki/Atlantic_Europe" title="Atlantic Europe">Atlantic Europe</a> to <a href="/wiki/Pacific_Asia" title="Pacific Asia">Pacific Asia</a> and the Indian Ocean respectively in the late 15th century.<sup id="cite&#95;ref-66" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-66"><span class="cite-bracket">&#91;</span>64<span class="cite-bracket">&#93;</span></a></sup> The <a href="/wiki/Russian_Empire" title="Russian Empire">Russian Empire</a> also began to expand into northwestern Asia from the 17th century, and would eventually take control of all of Siberia and most of Central Asia by the end of the 19th century.
</p><p>Among non-European empires, the <a href="/wiki/Ottoman_Empire" title="Ottoman Empire">Ottoman Empire</a> controlled Anatolia, most of the Middle East, North Africa and the Balkans from the mid 16th century onward, while in the 17th century, the <a href="/wiki/Manchu" class="mw-redirect" title="Manchu">Manchu</a> conquered China and established the <a href="/wiki/Qing_dynasty" title="Qing dynasty">Qing dynasty</a>. The Islamic <a href="/wiki/Mughal_Empire" title="Mughal Empire">Mughal Empire</a> (preceded by the <a href="/wiki/Delhi_Sultanate" title="Delhi Sultanate">Delhi Sultanate</a> of the 13th to early 16th century)<sup id="cite&#95;ref-67" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-67"><span class="cite-bracket">&#91;</span>65<span class="cite-bracket">&#93;</span></a></sup> and the Hindu <a href="/wiki/Maratha_Empire" title="Maratha Empire">Maratha Empire</a> controlled much of India in the 16th and 18th centuries respectively.<sup id="cite&#95;ref-68" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-68"><span class="cite-bracket">&#91;</span>66<span class="cite-bracket">&#93;</span></a></sup>
</p>

Pacific</a> and its Filipino colony (left) after the 1898 <a href="/wiki/Spanish%E2%80%93American_War" title="Spanish–American War">Spanish–American War</a></div></div></div></div></div>
<p><a href="/wiki/Western_imperialism_in_Asia" title="Western imperialism in Asia">Western imperialism in Asia</a> from the 18th to 20th centuries coincided with the <a href="/wiki/Industrial_Revolution" title="Industrial Revolution">Industrial Revolution</a> in the West and the dethroning of India and China as the world's foremost economies.<sup id="cite&#95;ref-69" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-69"><span class="cite-bracket">&#91;</span>67<span class="cite-bracket">&#93;</span></a></sup> The <a href="/wiki/British_Empire" title="British Empire">British Empire</a> first became dominant in South Asia, with most of the region being <a href="/wiki/Company_rule_in_India" title="Company rule in India">conquered by British traders</a> in the late 18th and early 19th centuries before falling under <a href="/wiki/British_Raj" title="British Raj">direct British rule</a> after a failed <a href="/wiki/1857_revolt" class="mw-redirect" title="1857 revolt">1857 revolt</a>; the 1869 completion of the <a href="/wiki/Suez_Canal" title="Suez Canal">Suez Canal</a>, which increased British access to India, went on to <a href="/wiki/New_Imperialism" title="New Imperialism">further European influence</a> over Africa and Asia.<sup id="cite&#95;ref-70" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-70"><span class="cite-bracket">&#91;</span>68<span class="cite-bracket">&#93;</span></a></sup> Around this time, Western powers started to dominate China in what later became known as the <a href="/wiki/Century_of_humiliation" title="Century of humiliation">century of humiliation</a>, with the British-supported <a href="/wiki/History_of_opium_in_China#Growth_of_the_opium_trade" title="History of opium in China">opium trade</a> and later <a href="/wiki/Opium_Wars" title="Opium Wars">Opium Wars</a> resulting in China being forced into an unprecedented situation of importing more than it exported.<sup id="cite&#95;ref-71" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-71"><span class="cite-bracket">&#91;</span>69<span class="cite-bracket">&#93;</span></a></sup><sup id="cite&#95;ref-72" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-72"><span class="cite-bracket">&#91;</span>70<span class="cite-bracket">&#93;</span></a></sup>
</p><p>Foreign domination of China was furthered by the <a href="/wiki/Japanese_colonial_empire" title="Japanese colonial empire">Japanese colonial empire</a>, which controlled some of East Asia and briefly much of Southeast Asia (which had earlier been <a href="/wiki/European_colonisation_of_Southeast_Asia" title="European colonisation of Southeast Asia">taken over</a> by the British, Dutch and French in the late 19th century),<sup id="cite&#95;ref-73" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-73"><span class="cite-bracket">&#91;</span>71<span class="cite-bracket">&#93;</span></a></sup> <a href="/wiki/New_Guinea" title="New Guinea">New Guinea</a> and the <a href="/wiki/Pacific_islands" class="mw-redirect" title="Pacific islands">Pacific islands</a>; Japan's domination was enabled by its rapid rise that had taken place during the <a href="/wiki/Meiji_era" title="Meiji era">Meiji era</a> of the late 19th century, in which it applied industrial knowledge learned from the West and thus overtook the rest of Asia.<sup id="cite&#95;ref-74" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-74"><span class="cite-bracket">&#91;</span>72<span class="cite-bracket">&#93;</span></a></sup><sup id="cite&#95;ref-75" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-75"><span class="cite-bracket">&#91;</span>73<span class="cite-bracket">&#93;</span></a></sup> One significant influence on Japan had been the United States, which had begun projecting influence across the Pacific after its early-to-mid-19th century <a href="/wiki/Westward_expansion" class="mw-redirect" title="Westward expansion">westward expansion</a>.<sup id="cite&#95;ref-76" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-76"><span class="cite-bracket">&#91;</span>74<span class="cite-bracket">&#93;</span></a></sup> The <a href="/wiki/Decline_of_the_Ottoman_Empire" class="mw-redirect" title="Decline of the Ottoman Empire">breakup of the Ottoman Empire</a> in the early 20th century led to the Middle East also being <a href="/wiki/History_of_the_Middle_East#Modern_Middle_East" title="History of the Middle East">contested and partitioned</a> by the British and French.<sup id="cite&#95;ref-77" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-77"><span class="cite-bracket">&#91;</span>75<span class="cite-bracket">&#93;</span></a></sup>
</p>
<div class="mw-heading mw-heading3"><h3 id="Contemporary_era">Contemporary era</h3></div>
<figure class="mw-default-size" typeof="mw:File/Thumb"><a href="/wiki/File:Soviet_Union_and_China_map_including_the_three_co-bordering_countries.svg" class="mw-file-description"><img src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/9/92/Soviet_Union_and_China_map_including_the_three_co-bordering_countries.svg/250px-Soviet_Union_and_China_map_including_the_three_co-bordering_countries.svg.png" decoding="async" width="250" height="133" class="mw-file-element" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/9/92/Soviet_Union_and_China_map_including_the_three_co-bordering_countries.svg/500px-Soviet_Union_and_China_map_including_the_three_co-bordering_countries.svg.png 1.5x" data-file-width="317" data-file-height="169" /></a><figcaption>The Soviet Union (in red) and China (yellow) controlled most of Asia in the late 20th century.</figcaption></figure>
<p>With the end of <a href="/wiki/World_War_II" title="World War II">World War II</a> in 1945 and the wartime ruination of Europe and imperial Japan, many countries in Asia were able to rapidly <a href="/wiki/Decolonisation_of_Asia" title="Decolonisation of Asia">free themselves</a> of colonial rule.<sup id="cite&#95;ref-78" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-78"><span class="cite-bracket">&#91;</span>76<span class="cite-bracket">&#93;</span></a></sup> The <a href="/wiki/Independence_of_India" class="mw-redirect" title="Independence of India">independence of India</a> came along with the <a href="/wiki/Partition_of_India" title="Partition of India">carving out</a> of a separate nation for the majority of <a href="/wiki/South_Asian_Muslims" class="mw-redirect" title="South Asian Muslims">South Asian Muslims</a>, which in 1971 <a href="/wiki/Bangladesh_Liberation_War" title="Bangladesh Liberation War">further split</a> into Pakistan and <a href="/wiki/Bangladesh" title="Bangladesh">Bangladesh</a>;<sup id="cite&#95;ref-79" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-79"><span class="cite-bracket">&#91;</span>77<span class="cite-bracket">&#93;</span></a></sup> The <a href="/wiki/Cold_War_in_Asia" title="Cold War in Asia">Cold War in Asia</a> strained relations between India and Pakistan and affected Asia more generally.<sup id="cite&#95;ref-80" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-80"><span class="cite-bracket">&#91;</span>78<span class="cite-bracket">&#93;</span></a></sup> The <a href="/wiki/End_of_the_Cold_War" class="mw-redirect" title="End of the Cold War">end of the Cold War</a> and the <a href="/wiki/Dissolution_of_the_Soviet_Union" title="Dissolution of the Soviet Union">Soviet Union</a> by 1991 saw the independence of the five modern Central Asian countries.<sup id="cite&#95;ref-81" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-81"><span class="cite-bracket">&#91;</span>79<span class="cite-bracket">&#93;</span></a></sup>
</p><p>Some Arab countries took economic advantage of massive oil deposits that were discovered in their territory, becoming globally influential,<sup id="cite&#95;ref-82" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-82"><span class="cite-bracket">&#91;</span>80<span class="cite-bracket">&#93;</span></a></sup> though stability in the Middle East has been affected since 1948 by the <a href="/wiki/Arab%E2%80%93Israeli_conflict" title="Arab–Israeli conflict">Arab–Israeli conflict</a> and American-led <a href="/wiki/United_States_foreign_policy_in_the_Middle_East" title="United States foreign policy in the Middle East">interventions</a>.<sup id="cite&#95;ref-83" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-83"><span class="cite-bracket">&#91;</span>81<span class="cite-bracket">&#93;</span></a></sup><sup id="cite&#95;ref-84" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-84"><span class="cite-bracket">&#91;</span>82<span class="cite-bracket">&#93;</span></a></sup> East Asian nations (along with Singapore in Southeast Asia) became economically prosperous with high-growth "<a href="/wiki/Tiger_economies" class="mw-redirect" title="Tiger economies">tiger economies</a>";<sup id="cite&#95;ref-85" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-85"><span class="cite-bracket">&#91;</span>83<span class="cite-bracket">&#93;</span></a></sup> China, having undergone the <a href="/wiki/Reform_and_opening_up" title="Reform and opening up">reform and opening up</a> under <a href="/wiki/Deng_Xiaoping" title="Deng Xiaoping">Deng Xiaoping</a>,<sup id="cite&#95;ref-86" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-86"><span class="cite-bracket">&#91;</span>84<span class="cite-bracket">&#93;</span></a></sup> regained its place among the top two economies of the world by the 21st century.<sup id="cite&#95;ref-87" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-87"><span class="cite-bracket">&#91;</span>85<span class="cite-bracket">&#93;</span></a></sup> India has also grown significantly because of <a href="/wiki/Economic_liberalisation_in_India" title="Economic liberalisation in India">economic liberalisation</a> that started in the 1990s,<sup id="cite&#95;ref-88" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-88"><span class="cite-bracket">&#91;</span>86<span class="cite-bracket">&#93;</span></a></sup> with extreme poverty now below 20%;<sup id="cite&#95;ref-89" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-89"><span class="cite-bracket">&#91;</span>87<span class="cite-bracket">&#93;</span></a></sup> India and China's rise has coincided with growing tensions between the two, with the <a href="/wiki/Indo-Pacific" title="Indo-Pacific">Indo-Pacific</a> now an actively contested area between China and counterbalancing forces.<sup id="cite&#95;ref-90" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-90"><span class="cite-bracket">&#91;</span>88<span class="cite-bracket">&#93;</span></a></sup><sup id="cite&#95;ref-91" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-91"><span class="cite-bracket">&#91;</span>89<span class="cite-bracket">&#93;</span></a></sup>
</p>
<ul class="gallery mw-gallery-traditional">
		<li class="gallerybox" style="width: 155px">

<div class="mw-heading mw-heading2"><h2 id="Geography">Geography</h2></div>
<link rel="mw-deduplicated-inline-style" href="mw-data:TemplateStyles:r1320445320" /><div role="note" class="hatnote navigation-not-searchable">Main article: <a href="/wiki/Geography_of_Asia" title="Geography of Asia">Geography of Asia</a></div>
<link rel="mw-deduplicated-inline-style" href="mw-data:TemplateStyles:r1320445320" /><div role="note" class="hatnote navigation-not-searchable">See also: <a href="/wiki/Category:Biota_of_Asia" title="Category:Biota of Asia">Category:Biota of Asia</a></div>
<figure class="mw-default-size" typeof="mw:File/Thumb"><a href="/wiki/File:Himalayas.jpg" class="mw-file-description"><img src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/7/79/Himalayas.jpg/250px-Himalayas.jpg" decoding="async" width="250" height="165" class="mw-file-element" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/7/79/Himalayas.jpg/500px-Himalayas.jpg 1.5x" data-file-width="3032" data-file-height="1998" /></a><figcaption>The <a href="/wiki/Himalayas" title="Himalayas">Himalayan</a> range is home to some of the planet's highest peaks.</figcaption></figure>
<p>Asia is the largest continent on Earth. It covers 9% of the Earth's total surface area (or 30% of its land area), and has the longest coastline, at 62,800 kilometres (39,022&#160;mi). Asia is generally defined as comprising the eastern four-fifths of Eurasia. It is located to the east of the <a href="/wiki/Suez_Canal" title="Suez Canal">Suez Canal</a> and the <a href="/wiki/Ural_Mountains" title="Ural Mountains">Ural Mountains</a>, and south of the <a href="/wiki/Caucasus_Mountains" title="Caucasus Mountains">Caucasus Mountains</a> (or the <a href="/wiki/Kuma%E2%80%93Manych_Depression" title="Kuma–Manych Depression">Kuma–Manych Depression</a>) and the <a href="/wiki/Caspian_Sea" title="Caspian Sea">Caspian</a> and <a href="/wiki/Black_Sea" title="Black Sea">Black Seas</a>.<sup id="cite&#95;ref-National&#95;Geographic-1999&#95;15-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-National_Geographic-1999-15"><span class="cite-bracket">&#91;</span>13<span class="cite-bracket">&#93;</span></a></sup><sup id="cite&#95;ref-93" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-93"><span class="cite-bracket">&#91;</span>91<span class="cite-bracket">&#93;</span></a></sup> It is bounded on the east by the <a href="/wiki/Pacific_Ocean" title="Pacific Ocean">Pacific Ocean</a>, on the south by the Indian Ocean and on the north by the Arctic Ocean. Asia is subdivided into 49 countries, five of them (<a href="/wiki/Georgia_(country)" title="Georgia (country)">Georgia</a>, <a href="/wiki/Azerbaijan" title="Azerbaijan">Azerbaijan</a>, <a href="/wiki/Russia" title="Russia">Russia</a>, <a href="/wiki/Kazakhstan" title="Kazakhstan">Kazakhstan</a> and <a href="/wiki/Turkey" title="Turkey">Turkey</a>) are <a href="/wiki/Transcontinental_countries" class="mw-redirect" title="Transcontinental countries">transcontinental countries</a> lying partly in Europe. Geographically, Russia is partly and mostly in Asia, but is considered a <a href="/wiki/List_of_sovereign_states_and_dependent_territories_in_Europe" title="List of sovereign states and dependent territories in Europe">European nation</a>, both <a href="/wiki/Russian_culture" class="mw-redirect" title="Russian culture">culturally</a> and politically.
</p><p>The <a href="/wiki/Gobi_Desert" title="Gobi Desert">Gobi Desert</a> is in Mongolia and the <a href="/wiki/Arabian_Desert" title="Arabian Desert">Arabian Desert</a> stretches across much of the Middle East. The <a href="/wiki/Yangtze" title="Yangtze">Yangtze</a> in China is the longest river in the continent. The Himalayas between Nepal and China is the tallest mountain range in the world. <a href="/wiki/Tropical_rainforest" title="Tropical rainforest">Tropical rainforests</a> stretch across much of southern Asia and coniferous and <a href="/wiki/Deciduous" title="Deciduous">deciduous</a> forests lie farther north.
</p>
<ul class="gallery mw-gallery-traditional">
