<div class="mw-heading mw-heading3"><h3 id="Main_regions">Main regions</h3></div>
<figure class="mw-default-size" typeof="mw:File/Thumb"><a href="/wiki/File:Detailed_map_of_Asian_regions.png" class="mw-file-description"><img src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/c/c5/Detailed_map_of_Asian_regions.png/500px-Detailed_map_of_Asian_regions.png" decoding="async" width="350" height="345" class="mw-file-element" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/c/c5/Detailed_map_of_Asian_regions.png/960px-Detailed_map_of_Asian_regions.png 1.5x" data-file-width="3465" data-file-height="3417" /></a><figcaption>Detailed map of Asian regions</figcaption></figure>
<p>There are various approaches to the regional division of Asia. The following subdivision into regions is used, among others, by the <a href="/wiki/United_Nations_Statistics_Division" title="United Nations Statistics Division">United Nations Statistics Division</a> (UNSD). This division of Asia into regions by the United Nations is done solely for statistical reasons and does not imply any assumption about political or other affiliations of countries and territories.<sup id="cite&#95;ref-94" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-94"><span class="cite-bracket">&#91;</span>92<span class="cite-bracket">&#93;</span></a></sup>
</p>
<ul><li><a href="/wiki/North_Asia" title="North Asia">North Asia</a> (<a href="/wiki/Siberia" title="Siberia">Siberia</a>)<sup id="cite&#95;ref-95" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-95"><span class="cite-bracket">&#91;</span>note 3<span class="cite-bracket">&#93;</span></a></sup></li>
<li><a href="/wiki/Central_Asia" title="Central Asia">Central Asia</a></li>
<li><a href="/wiki/West_Asia" title="West Asia">West Asia</a> (The <a href="/wiki/Middle_East" title="Middle East">Middle East</a> or <a href="/wiki/Near_East" title="Near East">Near East</a> and part of the <a href="/wiki/Caucasus" title="Caucasus">Caucasus</a>)</li>
<li><a href="/wiki/South_Asia" title="South Asia">South Asia</a> (<a href="/wiki/Indian_subcontinent" title="Indian subcontinent">Indian subcontinent</a>)</li>
<li><a href="/wiki/East_Asia" title="East Asia">East Asia</a> (<a href="/wiki/Far_East" title="Far East">Far East</a>)</li>
<li><a href="/wiki/Southeast_Asia" title="Southeast Asia">Southeast Asia</a> (<a href="/wiki/East_Indies" title="East Indies">East Indies</a> and <a href="/wiki/Indochina" class="mw-redirect" title="Indochina">Indochina</a>)</li></ul>
<div class="mw-heading mw-heading3"><h3 id="Climate">Climate</h3></div>
<link rel="mw-deduplicated-inline-style" href="mw-data:TemplateStyles:r1320445320" /><div role="note" class="hatnote navigation-not-searchable">Main article: <a href="/wiki/Climate_of_Asia" title="Climate of Asia">Climate of Asia</a></div>
<figure class="mw-default-size" typeof="mw:File/Thumb"><a href="/wiki/File:Koppen-Geiger_Map_v2_Asia_1991%E2%80%932020.svg" class="mw-file-description"><img src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/e/ed/Koppen-Geiger_Map_v2_Asia_1991%E2%80%932020.svg/250px-Koppen-Geiger_Map_v2_Asia_1991%E2%80%932020.svg.png" decoding="async" width="250" height="118" class="mw-file-element" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/e/ed/Koppen-Geiger_Map_v2_Asia_1991%E2%80%932020.svg/500px-Koppen-Geiger_Map_v2_Asia_1991%E2%80%932020.svg.png 1.5x" data-file-width="1803" data-file-height="850" /></a><figcaption><a href="/wiki/K%C3%B6ppen_climate_classification" title="Köppen climate classification">Köppen-Geiger climate classification</a> map for Asia<sup id="cite&#95;ref-96" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-96"><span class="cite-bracket">&#91;</span>93<span class="cite-bracket">&#93;</span></a></sup></figcaption></figure>
<p>Asia has extremely diverse climate features. Climates range from <a href="/wiki/Arctic" title="Arctic">Arctic</a> and <a href="/wiki/Subarctic" title="Subarctic">subarctic</a> in Siberia to tropical in southern India and Southeast Asia. It is moist across southeast sections, and dry across much of the interior. Some of the largest daily temperature ranges on Earth occur in western sections of Asia. The <a href="/wiki/Monsoon" title="Monsoon">monsoon</a> circulation dominates across southern and eastern sections, due to the presence of the Himalayas forcing the formation of a thermal low which draws in moisture during the summer. Southwestern sections of the continent are hot. Siberia is one of the coldest places in the <a href="/wiki/Northern_Hemisphere" title="Northern Hemisphere">Northern Hemisphere</a>, and can act as a source of arctic air masses for North America. The most active place on Earth for <a href="/wiki/Tropical_cyclone" title="Tropical cyclone">tropical cyclone</a> activity lies northeast of the <a href="/wiki/Philippines" title="Philippines">Philippines</a> and south of Japan.
</p>
<div class="mw-heading mw-heading4"><h4 id="Climate_change">Climate change</h4></div>
<div class="excerpt-block"><style data-mw-deduplicate="TemplateStyles:r1066933788">.mw-parser-output .excerpt-hat .mw-editsection-like{font-style:normal}</style><link rel="mw-deduplicated-inline-style" href="mw-data:TemplateStyles:r1320445320" /><div role="note" class="hatnote navigation-not-searchable dablink excerpt-hat selfref">This section is an excerpt from <a href="/wiki/Climate_change_in_Asia" title="Climate change in Asia">Climate change in Asia</a>.<span class="mw-editsection-like plainlinks"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a class="external text" href="https://en.wikipedia.org/w/index.php?title=Climate_change_in_Asia&amp;action=edit">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></div><div class="excerpt">
<figure class="mw-default-size" typeof="mw:File/Thumb"><a href="/wiki/File:2022_Pakistan_Floods_-_August_27,_2021_vs._August_27,_2022_in_Sindh.jpg" class="mw-file-description"><img src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/5/53/2022_Pakistan_Floods_-_August_27%2C_2021_vs._August_27%2C_2022_in_Sindh.jpg/500px-2022_Pakistan_Floods_-_August_27%2C_2021_vs._August_27%2C_2022_in_Sindh.jpg" decoding="async" width="500" height="328" class="mw-file-element" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/5/53/2022_Pakistan_Floods_-_August_27%2C_2021_vs._August_27%2C_2022_in_Sindh.jpg/960px-2022_Pakistan_Floods_-_August_27%2C_2021_vs._August_27%2C_2022_in_Sindh.jpg 1.5x, //upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/5/53/2022_Pakistan_Floods_-_August_27%2C_2021_vs._August_27%2C_2022_in_Sindh.jpg/1280px-2022_Pakistan_Floods_-_August_27%2C_2021_vs._August_27%2C_2022_in_Sindh.jpg 2x" data-file-width="8830" data-file-height="5792" /></a><figcaption>The <a href="/wiki/2022_South_Asian_floods" title="2022 South Asian floods">2022 South Asian floods</a>, including in <a href="/wiki/2022_Pakistan_floods" title="2022 Pakistan floods">Pakistan</a> (pictured), are an example of a climate change impact.<sup id="cite&#95;ref-97" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-97"><span class="cite-bracket">&#91;</span>94<span class="cite-bracket">&#93;</span></a></sup><sup id="cite&#95;ref-98" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-98"><span class="cite-bracket">&#91;</span>95<span class="cite-bracket">&#93;</span></a></sup></figcaption></figure>
<p><a href="/wiki/Climate_change" title="Climate change">Climate change</a> is particularly important in Asia, as the continent accounts for the majority of the human population. Warming since the 20th century is increasing the threat of <a href="/wiki/Heatwave" class="mw-redirect" title="Heatwave">heatwaves</a> across the entire continent.<sup id="cite&#95;ref-Climate&#95;change&#95;in&#95;Asia&#95;AR6&#95;WGII&#95;Chapter10&#95;99-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Climate_change_in_Asia_AR6_WGII_Chapter10-99"><span class="cite-bracket">&#91;</span>96<span class="cite-bracket">&#93;</span></a></sup><sup class="reference nowrap"><span title="Page / location: 1459">&#58;&#8202;1459&#8202;</span></sup> Heatwaves lead to increased mortality, and the demand for <a href="/wiki/Air_conditioning" title="Air conditioning">air conditioning</a> is rapidly accelerating as the result. By 2080, around 1 billion people in the cities of <a href="/wiki/South_Asia" title="South Asia">South</a> and <a href="/wiki/Southeast_Asia" title="Southeast Asia">Southeast Asia</a> are expected to experience around a month of extreme heat every year.<sup id="cite&#95;ref-Climate&#95;change&#95;in&#95;Asia&#95;AR6&#95;WGII&#95;Chapter10&#95;99-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Climate_change_in_Asia_AR6_WGII_Chapter10-99"><span class="cite-bracket">&#91;</span>96<span class="cite-bracket">&#93;</span></a></sup><sup class="reference nowrap"><span title="Page / location: 1460">&#58;&#8202;1460&#8202;</span></sup> The <a href="/wiki/Effects_of_climate_change_on_the_water_cycle" title="Effects of climate change on the water cycle">impacts on water cycle</a> are more complicated: already arid regions, primarily located in <a href="/wiki/West_Asia" title="West Asia">West Asia</a> and <a href="/wiki/Central_Asia" title="Central Asia">Central Asia</a>, will see more <a href="/wiki/Drought" title="Drought">droughts</a>, while areas of <a href="/wiki/East_Asia" title="East Asia">East</a>, Southeast and South Asia which are already wet due to the <a href="/wiki/Monsoon" title="Monsoon">monsoons</a> will experience more flooding.<sup id="cite&#95;ref-Climate&#95;change&#95;in&#95;Asia&#95;AR6&#95;WGII&#95;Chapter10&#95;99-2" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Climate_change_in_Asia_AR6_WGII_Chapter10-99"><span class="cite-bracket">&#91;</span>96<span class="cite-bracket">&#93;</span></a></sup><sup class="reference nowrap"><span title="Page / location: 1459">&#58;&#8202;1459&#8202;</span></sup>
</p><p>The waters around Asia are subjected to the same <a href="/wiki/Effects_of_climate_change_on_oceans" title="Effects of climate change on oceans">impacts</a> as elsewhere, such as the increased warming and <a href="/wiki/Ocean_acidification" title="Ocean acidification">ocean acidification</a>.<sup id="cite&#95;ref-Climate&#95;change&#95;in&#95;Asia&#95;AR6&#95;WGII&#95;Chapter10&#95;99-3" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Climate_change_in_Asia_AR6_WGII_Chapter10-99"><span class="cite-bracket">&#91;</span>96<span class="cite-bracket">&#93;</span></a></sup><sup class="reference nowrap"><span title="Page / location: 1465">&#58;&#8202;1465&#8202;</span></sup> There are many <a href="/wiki/Coral_reef" title="Coral reef">coral reefs</a> in the region, and they are highly vulnerable to climate change,<sup id="cite&#95;ref-Climate&#95;change&#95;in&#95;Asia&#95;AR6&#95;WGII&#95;Chapter10&#95;99-4" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Climate_change_in_Asia_AR6_WGII_Chapter10-99"><span class="cite-bracket">&#91;</span>96<span class="cite-bracket">&#93;</span></a></sup><sup class="reference nowrap"><span title="Page / location: 1459">&#58;&#8202;1459&#8202;</span></sup> to the point practically all of them will be lost if the warming exceeds 1.5&#160;°C (2.7&#160;°F).<sup id="cite&#95;ref-Climate&#95;change&#95;in&#95;Asia&#95;ArmstrongMcKay2022&#95;100-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Climate_change_in_Asia_ArmstrongMcKay2022-100"><span class="cite-bracket">&#91;</span>97<span class="cite-bracket">&#93;</span></a></sup><sup id="cite&#95;ref-Climate&#95;change&#95;in&#95;Asia&#95;ArmstrongMcKayExplainer&#95;101-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Climate_change_in_Asia_ArmstrongMcKayExplainer-101"><span class="cite-bracket">&#91;</span>98<span class="cite-bracket">&#93;</span></a></sup> Asia's distinctive <a href="/wiki/Mangrove" title="Mangrove">mangrove</a> ecosystems are also highly vulnerable to <a href="/wiki/Sea_level_rise" title="Sea level rise">sea level rise</a>.<sup id="cite&#95;ref-Climate&#95;change&#95;in&#95;Asia&#95;AR6&#95;WGII&#95;Chapter10&#95;99-5" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Climate_change_in_Asia_AR6_WGII_Chapter10-99"><span class="cite-bracket">&#91;</span>96<span class="cite-bracket">&#93;</span></a></sup><sup class="reference nowrap"><span title="Page / location: 1459">&#58;&#8202;1459&#8202;</span></sup> Asia also has more countries with large coastal populations than any other continent, which would cause large economic impacts from sea level rise.<sup id="cite&#95;ref-Climate&#95;change&#95;in&#95;Asia&#95;AR6&#95;WGII&#95;Chapter10&#95;99-6" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Climate_change_in_Asia_AR6_WGII_Chapter10-99"><span class="cite-bracket">&#91;</span>96<span class="cite-bracket">&#93;</span></a></sup><sup class="reference nowrap"><span title="Page / location: 1459">&#58;&#8202;1459&#8202;</span></sup> Water supplies in the <a href="/wiki/Hindu_Kush" title="Hindu Kush">Hindu Kush</a> region will become more unstable as its enormous <a href="/wiki/Glacier" title="Glacier">glaciers</a>, known as the "Asian water towers", gradually melt.<sup id="cite&#95;ref-Climate&#95;change&#95;in&#95;Asia&#95;AR6&#95;WGII&#95;Chapter10&#95;99-7" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Climate_change_in_Asia_AR6_WGII_Chapter10-99"><span class="cite-bracket">&#91;</span>96<span class="cite-bracket">&#93;</span></a></sup><sup class="reference nowrap"><span title="Page / location: 1459">&#58;&#8202;1459&#8202;</span></sup> These changes to water cycle also affect vector-borne disease distribution, with malaria and dengue fever expected to become more prominent in the tropical and subtropical regions.<sup id="cite&#95;ref-Climate&#95;change&#95;in&#95;Asia&#95;AR6&#95;WGII&#95;Chapter10&#95;99-8" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Climate_change_in_Asia_AR6_WGII_Chapter10-99"><span class="cite-bracket">&#91;</span>96<span class="cite-bracket">&#93;</span></a></sup><sup class="reference nowrap"><span title="Page / location: 1459">&#58;&#8202;1459&#8202;</span></sup> <a href="/wiki/Food_security" title="Food security">Food security</a> will become more uneven, and South Asian countries could experience significant impacts from global food price volatility.<sup id="cite&#95;ref-Climate&#95;change&#95;in&#95;Asia&#95;AR6&#95;WGII&#95;Chapter10&#95;99-9" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Climate_change_in_Asia_AR6_WGII_Chapter10-99"><span class="cite-bracket">&#91;</span>96<span class="cite-bracket">&#93;</span></a></sup><sup class="reference nowrap"><span title="Page / location: 1494">&#58;&#8202;1494&#8202;</span></sup>
</p>
<figure class="mw-default-size" typeof="mw:File/Thumb"><a href="/wiki/File:Kang_2018_NCP_irrigation_RCPs.png" class="mw-file-description"><img src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/6/6c/Kang_2018_NCP_irrigation_RCPs.png/250px-Kang_2018_NCP_irrigation_RCPs.png" decoding="async" width="250" height="278" class="mw-file-element" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/6/6c/Kang_2018_NCP_irrigation_RCPs.png/500px-Kang_2018_NCP_irrigation_RCPs.png 1.5x" data-file-width="2049" data-file-height="2282" /></a><figcaption>Climate change is expected to exacerbate <a href="/wiki/Heat_stress" class="mw-redirect" title="Heat stress">heat stress</a> over at the <a href="/wiki/North_China_Plain" title="North China Plain">North China Plain</a>, which is particularly vulnerable as widespread <a href="/wiki/Irrigation" title="Irrigation">irrigation</a> results in very moist air. There is a risk that agricultural labourers will be physically unable to work outdoors on hot summer days at the end of the century, particularly under the scenario of greatest emissions and warming.<sup id="cite&#95;ref-Climate&#95;change&#95;in&#95;Asia&#95;Kang2018&#95;102-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Climate_change_in_Asia_Kang2018-102"><span class="cite-bracket">&#91;</span>99<span class="cite-bracket">&#93;</span></a></sup></figcaption></figure>
<p>Historical emissions from Asia are lower than those from Europe and North America. However, China has been the single largest emitter of greenhouse gases in the 21st century, while India is the third-largest. As a whole, Asia currently accounts for 36% of world's primary <a href="/wiki/Energy" title="Energy">energy</a> consumption, which is expected to increase to 48% by 2050. By 2040, it is also expected to account for 80% of the world's <a href="/wiki/Coal" title="Coal">coal</a> and 26% of the world's <a href="/wiki/Natural_gas" title="Natural gas">natural gas</a> consumption.<sup id="cite&#95;ref-Climate&#95;change&#95;in&#95;Asia&#95;AR6&#95;WGII&#95;Chapter10&#95;99-10" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Climate_change_in_Asia_AR6_WGII_Chapter10-99"><span class="cite-bracket">&#91;</span>96<span class="cite-bracket">&#93;</span></a></sup><sup class="reference nowrap"><span title="Page / location: 1468">&#58;&#8202;1468&#8202;</span></sup> While the <a href="/wiki/United_States" title="United States">United States</a> remains the world's largest <a href="/wiki/Oil" title="Oil">oil</a> consumer, by 2050 it is projected to move to third place, behind China and India.<sup id="cite&#95;ref-Climate&#95;change&#95;in&#95;Asia&#95;AR6&#95;WGII&#95;Chapter10&#95;99-11" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Climate_change_in_Asia_AR6_WGII_Chapter10-99"><span class="cite-bracket">&#91;</span>96<span class="cite-bracket">&#93;</span></a></sup><sup class="reference nowrap"><span title="Page / location: 1470">&#58;&#8202;1470&#8202;</span></sup> While nearly half of the world's new <a href="/wiki/Renewable_energy" title="Renewable energy">renewable energy</a> capacity is built in Asia,<sup id="cite&#95;ref-Climate&#95;change&#95;in&#95;Asia&#95;AR6&#95;WGII&#95;Chapter10&#95;99-12" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Climate_change_in_Asia_AR6_WGII_Chapter10-99"><span class="cite-bracket">&#91;</span>96<span class="cite-bracket">&#93;</span></a></sup><sup class="reference nowrap"><span title="Page / location: 1470">&#58;&#8202;1470&#8202;</span></sup> this is not yet sufficient in order to meet the goals of the <a href="/wiki/Paris_Agreement" title="Paris Agreement">Paris Agreement</a>. They imply that the renewables would account for 35% of total energy consumption in Asia by 2030.<sup id="cite&#95;ref-Climate&#95;change&#95;in&#95;Asia&#95;AR6&#95;WGII&#95;Chapter10&#95;99-13" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Climate_change_in_Asia_AR6_WGII_Chapter10-99"><span class="cite-bracket">&#91;</span>96<span class="cite-bracket">&#93;</span></a></sup><sup class="reference nowrap"><span title="Page / location: 1471">&#58;&#8202;1471&#8202;</span></sup>
</p><p><a href="/wiki/Climate_change_adaptation" title="Climate change adaptation">Climate change adaptation</a> is already a reality for many Asian countries, with a wide range of strategies attempted across the continent.<sup id="cite&#95;ref-Climate&#95;change&#95;in&#95;Asia&#95;AR6&#95;WGII&#95;Chapter10&#95;99-14" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Climate_change_in_Asia_AR6_WGII_Chapter10-99"><span class="cite-bracket">&#91;</span>96<span class="cite-bracket">&#93;</span></a></sup><sup class="reference nowrap"><span title="Page / location: 1534">&#58;&#8202;1534&#8202;</span></sup> Important examples include the growing implementation of <a href="/wiki/Climate-smart_agriculture" title="Climate-smart agriculture">climate-smart agriculture</a> in certain countries or the "<a href="/wiki/Sponge_city" title="Sponge city">sponge city</a>" planning principles in China.<sup id="cite&#95;ref-Climate&#95;change&#95;in&#95;Asia&#95;AR6&#95;WGII&#95;Chapter10&#95;99-15" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Climate_change_in_Asia_AR6_WGII_Chapter10-99"><span class="cite-bracket">&#91;</span>96<span class="cite-bracket">&#93;</span></a></sup><sup class="reference nowrap"><span title="Page / location: 1534">&#58;&#8202;1534&#8202;</span></sup> While some countries have drawn up extensive frameworks such as the Bangladesh Delta Plan or Japan's Climate Adaptation Act,<sup id="cite&#95;ref-Climate&#95;change&#95;in&#95;Asia&#95;AR6&#95;WGII&#95;Chapter10&#95;99-16" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Climate_change_in_Asia_AR6_WGII_Chapter10-99"><span class="cite-bracket">&#91;</span>96<span class="cite-bracket">&#93;</span></a></sup><sup class="reference nowrap"><span title="Page / location: 1508">&#58;&#8202;1508&#8202;</span></sup> others still rely on localized actions that are not effectively scaled up.<sup id="cite&#95;ref-Climate&#95;change&#95;in&#95;Asia&#95;AR6&#95;WGII&#95;Chapter10&#95;99-17" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Climate_change_in_Asia_AR6_WGII_Chapter10-99"><span class="cite-bracket">&#91;</span>96<span class="cite-bracket">&#93;</span></a></sup><sup class="reference nowrap"><span title="Page / location: 1534">&#58;&#8202;1534&#8202;</span></sup>
</p>
</div></div>
<div style="clear:both;" class=""></div>
<div class="mw-heading mw-heading2"><h2 id="Politics">Politics</h2></div>
<link rel="mw-deduplicated-inline-style" href="mw-data:TemplateStyles:r1320445320" /><div role="note" class="hatnote navigation-not-searchable">Main article: <a href="/wiki/Politics_of_Asia" title="Politics of Asia">Politics of Asia</a></div>

<p>The most <a href="/wiki/Democracy_in_Asia" title="Democracy in Asia">democratic countries in Asia</a> are <a href="/wiki/Japan" title="Japan">Japan</a>, <a href="/wiki/Taiwan" title="Taiwan">Taiwan</a> and <a href="/wiki/South_Korea" title="South Korea">South Korea</a> according to the <a href="/wiki/V-Dem_Democracy_indices" class="mw-redirect" title="V-Dem Democracy indices">V-Dem Democracy indices</a>.<sup id="cite&#95;ref-j496&#95;103-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-j496-103"><span class="cite-bracket">&#91;</span>100<span class="cite-bracket">&#93;</span></a></sup>
</p>

None of them are members of the <a href="/wiki/UN" class="mw-redirect" title="UN">UN</a>, however Palestine has <a href="/wiki/United_Nations_General_Assembly_observers" title="United Nations General Assembly observers">observer state status</a>:

<div style="clear:both;" class=""></div>
<div class="mw-heading mw-heading2"><h2 id="Economy">Economy</h2></div>
<link rel="mw-deduplicated-inline-style" href="mw-data:TemplateStyles:r1320445320" /><div role="note" class="hatnote navigation-not-searchable">Main articles: <a href="/wiki/Economy_of_Asia" title="Economy of Asia">Economy of Asia</a>, <a href="/wiki/List_of_Asian_countries_by_GDP" title="List of Asian countries by GDP">List of Asian countries by GDP</a>, <a href="/wiki/List_of_countries_in_Asia-Pacific_by_GDP_(nominal)" title="List of countries in Asia-Pacific by GDP (nominal)">List of countries in Asia-Pacific by GDP (nominal)</a>, and <a href="/wiki/List_of_Asian_and_Pacific_countries_by_GDP_(PPP)" title="List of Asian and Pacific countries by GDP (PPP)">List of Asian and Pacific countries by GDP (PPP)</a></div>
<figure class="mw-default-size" typeof="mw:File/Thumb"><a href="/wiki/File:1_Singapore_city_skyline_2010_day_panorama.jpg" class="mw-file-description"><img src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/8/82/1_Singapore_city_skyline_2010_day_panorama.jpg/330px-1_Singapore_city_skyline_2010_day_panorama.jpg" decoding="async" width="300" height="127" class="mw-file-element" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/8/82/1_Singapore_city_skyline_2010_day_panorama.jpg/500px-1_Singapore_city_skyline_2010_day_panorama.jpg 1.5x, //upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/8/82/1_Singapore_city_skyline_2010_day_panorama.jpg/960px-1_Singapore_city_skyline_2010_day_panorama.jpg 2x" data-file-width="4036" data-file-height="1703" /></a><figcaption>Singapore has one of the <a href="/wiki/List_of_busiest_container_ports" title="List of busiest container ports">busiest container ports in the world</a> and is the world's fourth-largest <a href="/wiki/Foreign_exchange" class="mw-redirect" title="Foreign exchange">foreign exchange</a> trading hub.</figcaption></figure>
<p>Asia is both the <a href="/wiki/List_of_continents_by_GDP" title="List of continents by GDP">largest continental economy</a> in the world by both <a href="/wiki/List_of_countries_by_GDP_(nominal)" title="List of countries by GDP (nominal)">GDP nominal</a> and <a href="/wiki/Purchasing_power_parity" title="Purchasing power parity">PPP</a> values and the world's fastest growing economic region.<sup id="cite&#95;ref-International&#95;Monetary&#95;Fund&#95;114-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-International_Monetary_Fund-114"><span class="cite-bracket">&#91;</span>101<span class="cite-bracket">&#93;</span></a></sup> As of 2025<sup class="plainlinks noexcerpt noprint asof-tag update" style="display:none;"><a class="external text" href="https://en.wikipedia.org/w/index.php?title=Asia&amp;action=edit">&#91;update&#93;</a></sup>, <a href="/wiki/China" title="China">China</a> is by far the <a href="/wiki/List_of_Asian_countries_by_GDP" title="List of Asian countries by GDP">largest economy</a> on the continent. It is followed by India, Japan, South Korea, Turkey, Indonesia, Saudi Arabia, and Taiwan, which are all ranked among the top 20 largest economies by both nominal GDP and PPP values.<sup id="cite&#95;ref-115" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-115"><span class="cite-bracket">&#91;</span>102<span class="cite-bracket">&#93;</span></a></sup> Based on Global Office Locations 2011, Asia dominated global office locations with 4 of the top 5 being in Asia: Hong Kong, Singapore, <a href="/wiki/Tokyo" title="Tokyo">Tokyo</a> and <a href="/wiki/Seoul" title="Seoul">Seoul</a>. Around 68% of international firms have an office in Hong Kong.<sup id="cite&#95;ref-116" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-116"><span class="cite-bracket">&#91;</span>103<span class="cite-bracket">&#93;</span></a></sup>
</p><p>In the late 1990s and early 2000s, the economy of China<sup id="cite&#95;ref-117" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-117"><span class="cite-bracket">&#91;</span>104<span class="cite-bracket">&#93;</span></a></sup> had an average annual growth rate of more than 8%. According to economic historian <a href="/wiki/Angus_Maddison" title="Angus Maddison">Angus Maddison</a>, India had the world's largest economy for much of the past three millennia prior to the 19th century, accounting for 25% of the world's industrial output.<sup id="cite&#95;ref-118" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-118"><span class="cite-bracket">&#91;</span>105<span class="cite-bracket">&#93;</span></a></sup><sup id="cite&#95;ref-119" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-119"><span class="cite-bracket">&#91;</span>106<span class="cite-bracket">&#93;</span></a></sup><sup id="cite&#95;ref-120" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-120"><span class="cite-bracket">&#91;</span>107<span class="cite-bracket">&#93;</span></a></sup><sup id="cite&#95;ref-121" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-121"><span class="cite-bracket">&#91;</span>108<span class="cite-bracket">&#93;</span></a></sup> China was the <a href="/wiki/Economic_history_of_China" title="Economic history of China">largest and most advanced economy</a> on Earth for much of recorded history and shared the mantle with India.<sup id="cite&#95;ref-122" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-122"><span class="cite-bracket">&#91;</span>109<span class="cite-bracket">&#93;</span></a></sup><sup id="cite&#95;ref-Eric.ed.gov-2000&#95;17-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Eric.ed.gov-2000-17"><span class="cite-bracket">&#91;</span>15<span class="cite-bracket">&#93;</span></a></sup><sup id="cite&#95;ref-123" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-123"><span class="cite-bracket">&#91;</span>110<span class="cite-bracket">&#93;</span></a></sup> For several decades in the late twentieth century, Japan was the largest economy in Asia and second-largest in the world, after surpassing the <a href="/wiki/Soviet_Union" title="Soviet Union">Soviet Union</a> (measured in net material product) in 1990 and Germany in 1968. This ended in 2010, when China overtook Japan to become the world's second largest economy. In 2025, India overtook Japan in terms of nominal GDP to become the <a href="/wiki/List_of_countries_by_GDP_(nominal)" title="List of countries by GDP (nominal)">world's 4th largest</a> and <a href="/wiki/List_of_Asian_countries_by_GDP" title="List of Asian countries by GDP">Asia's 2nd largest</a> economy.
</p><p>In the late 1980s and early 1990s, Japan's GDP by currency exchange rates was almost as large as that of the rest of Asia combined.<sup id="cite&#95;ref-International&#95;Monetary&#95;Fund&#95;114-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-International_Monetary_Fund-114"><span class="cite-bracket">&#91;</span>101<span class="cite-bracket">&#93;</span></a></sup> In 1995, Japan's economy nearly equaled that of the US as the largest economy in the world for a day, after the Japanese currency reached a record high of 79 <a href="/wiki/Yen" class="mw-redirect" title="Yen">yen</a>/US$. Economic growth in Asia since <a href="/wiki/World_War_II" title="World War II">World War II</a> to the 1990s had been concentrated in Japan as well as South Korea, Taiwan, Hong Kong, and Singapore, known as the <a href="/wiki/Four_Asian_Tigers" title="Four Asian Tigers">Four Asian Tigers</a>, which are now all considered developed economies, having among the highest GDP per capita in Asia.<sup id="cite&#95;ref-124" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-124"><span class="cite-bracket">&#91;</span>111<span class="cite-bracket">&#93;</span></a></sup><sup id="cite&#95;ref-International&#95;Monetary&#95;Fund&#95;114-2" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-International_Monetary_Fund-114"><span class="cite-bracket">&#91;</span>101<span class="cite-bracket">&#93;</span></a></sup>
</p>
<figure class="mw-default-size" typeof="mw:File/Thumb"><a href="/wiki/File:Mumbai_skyline_BWSL.jpg" class="mw-file-description"><img alt="" src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/b/bc/Mumbai_skyline_BWSL.jpg/250px-Mumbai_skyline_BWSL.jpg" decoding="async" width="250" height="198" class="mw-file-element" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/b/bc/Mumbai_skyline_BWSL.jpg/500px-Mumbai_skyline_BWSL.jpg 1.5x" data-file-width="707" data-file-height="560" /></a><figcaption>Mumbai is one of the most populous cities in Asia, as well as an economic and tourism hub.</figcaption></figure>
<p>Asia is the largest continent in the world by a considerable margin, and it is rich in natural resources, such as <a href="/wiki/Petroleum" title="Petroleum">petroleum</a>, <a href="/wiki/Forest" title="Forest">forests</a>, <a href="/wiki/Fish" title="Fish">fish</a>, <a href="/wiki/Water" title="Water">water</a>, <a href="/wiki/Rice" title="Rice">rice</a>, <a href="/wiki/Copper" title="Copper">copper</a> and <a href="/wiki/Silver" title="Silver">silver</a>. Manufacturing in Asia has traditionally been strongest in East and Southeast Asia, particularly in China, <a href="/wiki/Taiwan" title="Taiwan">Taiwan</a>, South Korea, Japan, India, the Philippines, and Singapore. Japan and South Korea continue to dominate in the area of <a href="/wiki/Multinational_corporation" title="Multinational corporation">multinational corporations</a>, though increasingly China and India are making significant inroads. Many companies from Europe, North America, South Korea, and Japan have operations in Asia's developing countries to take advantage of its abundant supply of cheap labour and relatively developed infrastructure.<sup id="cite&#95;ref-125" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-125"><span class="cite-bracket">&#91;</span>112<span class="cite-bracket">&#93;</span></a></sup><sup id="cite&#95;ref-126" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-126"><span class="cite-bracket">&#91;</span>113<span class="cite-bracket">&#93;</span></a></sup>
</p><p>According to <a href="/wiki/Citigroup" title="Citigroup">Citigroup</a> in 2011, 9 of 11 <a href="/wiki/3G_countries" title="3G countries">Global Growth Generator</a> countries came from Asia driven by population and income growth. They are Bangladesh, China, India, Indonesia, Iraq, Mongolia, the Philippines, Sri Lanka, and Vietnam.<sup id="cite&#95;ref-127" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-127"><span class="cite-bracket">&#91;</span>114<span class="cite-bracket">&#93;</span></a></sup> Asia has three main financial centers: Hong Kong, Tokyo, and Singapore. <a href="/wiki/Call_center" class="mw-redirect" title="Call center">Call centers</a> and <a href="/wiki/Business_process_outsourcing" title="Business process outsourcing">business process outsourcing</a> firms (BPOs) are becoming major employers in India and the Philippines due to the availability of a large pool of highly skilled, English-speaking workers. The increased use of outsourcing has assisted the rise of India and the China as financial centers. Due to its large and extremely competitive information technology industry, India has become a major hub for outsourcing.<sup id="cite&#95;ref-128" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-128"><span class="cite-bracket">&#91;</span>115<span class="cite-bracket">&#93;</span></a></sup>
</p><p>Trade between Asian countries and countries on other continents is largely carried out via sea routes. The main route leads from the Chinese coast south via Hanoi to Jakarta, Singapore, and Kuala Lumpur, through the <a href="/wiki/Strait_of_Malacca" title="Strait of Malacca">Strait of Malacca</a> via <a href="/wiki/Colombo" title="Colombo">Colombo</a> to the southern tip of India, then via Malé to  <a href="/wiki/Mombasa" title="Mombasa">Mombasa</a> in East Africa (see also <a href="/wiki/Indo-Pacific" title="Indo-Pacific">Indo-Pacific</a>), from there to <a href="/wiki/Djibouti" title="Djibouti">Djibouti</a>, then through the Red Sea and the <a href="/wiki/Suez_Canal" title="Suez Canal">Suez Canal</a> into the Mediterranean (see also: <a href="/wiki/Indo-Mediterranean" title="Indo-Mediterranean">Indo-Mediterranean</a>), then via Haifa, Istanbul, and <a href="/wiki/Athens" title="Athens">Athens</a> to the northern Italian hub of <a href="/wiki/Trieste" title="Trieste">Trieste</a>, with its rail connections to Central and Eastern Europe, or further to <a href="/wiki/Barcelona" title="Barcelona">Barcelona</a> and around Spain and France to northern European ports. A far smaller part of the goods traffic runs via <a href="/wiki/Cape_Route" title="Cape Route">South Africa to Europe</a>. A particularly significant part of Asian goods traffic is carried out on the <a href="/wiki/Pacific_Rim" title="Pacific Rim">Pacific Rim</a>, toward <a href="/wiki/Los_Angeles" title="Los Angeles">Los Angeles</a> and <a href="/wiki/Long_Beach" class="mw-redirect" title="Long Beach">Long Beach</a>. The melting of the Arctic is also paving the way for <a href="/wiki/Arctic_shipping_routes" title="Arctic shipping routes">new shipping routes</a> from Northeast Asia to Europe and North America.<sup id="cite&#95;ref-129" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-129"><span class="cite-bracket">&#91;</span>116<span class="cite-bracket">&#93;</span></a></sup> The land route to Europe is also the subject of construction projects, comparatively smaller in scope. Intra-Asian trade, including sea trade, is growing rapidly.<sup id="cite&#95;ref-130" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-130"><span class="cite-bracket">&#91;</span>117<span class="cite-bracket">&#93;</span></a></sup><sup id="cite&#95;ref-131" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-131"><span class="cite-bracket">&#91;</span>118<span class="cite-bracket">&#93;</span></a></sup><sup id="cite&#95;ref-132" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-132"><span class="cite-bracket">&#91;</span>119<span class="cite-bracket">&#93;</span></a></sup><sup id="cite&#95;ref-133" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-133"><span class="cite-bracket">&#91;</span>120<span class="cite-bracket">&#93;</span></a></sup><sup id="cite&#95;ref-134" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-134"><span class="cite-bracket">&#91;</span>121<span class="cite-bracket">&#93;</span></a></sup><sup id="cite&#95;ref-135" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-135"><span class="cite-bracket">&#91;</span>122<span class="cite-bracket">&#93;</span></a></sup>
</p><p>In 2010, Asia had 3.3&#160;million millionaires (people with net worth over US$1 million excluding their homes), slightly below North America's 3.4 million. In 2011, Asia topped Europe in the number of millionaires.<sup id="cite&#95;ref-136" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-136"><span class="cite-bracket">&#91;</span>123<span class="cite-bracket">&#93;</span></a></sup>
Citigroup in The Wealth Report 2012 stated that the total wealth of people in Asia with over $100 million in assets exceeded that of their North American counterparts for the first time, as the world's "economic center of gravity" continued moving east. At the end of 2011, there were 18,000 people in Asia, mainly in Southeast Asia, China, and Japan, with at least $100 million in disposable assets, compared to North America with 17,000 and Western Europe with 14,000.<sup id="cite&#95;ref-137" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-137"><span class="cite-bracket">&#91;</span>124<span class="cite-bracket">&#93;</span></a></sup>
</p>

<div style="clear:both;" class=""></div>
<div class="mw-heading mw-heading3"><h3 id="Tourism">Tourism</h3></div>
<figure class="mw-default-size" typeof="mw:File/Thumb"><a href="/wiki/File:%E0%B8%A7%E0%B8%B1%E0%B8%94%E0%B8%9E%E0%B8%A3%E0%B8%B0%E0%B8%A8%E0%B8%A3%E0%B8%B5%E0%B8%A3%E0%B8%B1%E0%B8%95%E0%B8%99%E0%B8%A8%E0%B8%B2%E0%B8%AA%E0%B8%94%E0%B8%B2%E0%B8%A3%E0%B8%B2%E0%B8%A1-5.jpg" class="mw-file-description"><img alt="A Thai temple complex with several ornate buildings, and a lot of visitors" src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/0/08/%E0%B8%A7%E0%B8%B1%E0%B8%94%E0%B8%9E%E0%B8%A3%E0%B8%B0%E0%B8%A8%E0%B8%A3%E0%B8%B5%E0%B8%A3%E0%B8%B1%E0%B8%95%E0%B8%99%E0%B8%A8%E0%B8%B2%E0%B8%AA%E0%B8%94%E0%B8%B2%E0%B8%A3%E0%B8%B2%E0%B8%A1-5.jpg/250px-%E0%B8%A7%E0%B8%B1%E0%B8%94%E0%B8%9E%E0%B8%A3%E0%B8%B0%E0%B8%A8%E0%B8%A3%E0%B8%B5%E0%B8%A3%E0%B8%B1%E0%B8%95%E0%B8%99%E0%B8%A8%E0%B8%B2%E0%B8%AA%E0%B8%94%E0%B8%B2%E0%B8%A3%E0%B8%B2%E0%B8%A1-5.jpg" decoding="async" width="250" height="141" class="mw-file-element" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/0/08/%E0%B8%A7%E0%B8%B1%E0%B8%94%E0%B8%9E%E0%B8%A3%E0%B8%B0%E0%B8%A8%E0%B8%A3%E0%B8%B5%E0%B8%A3%E0%B8%B1%E0%B8%95%E0%B8%99%E0%B8%A8%E0%B8%B2%E0%B8%AA%E0%B8%94%E0%B8%B2%E0%B8%A3%E0%B8%B2%E0%B8%A1-5.jpg/500px-%E0%B8%A7%E0%B8%B1%E0%B8%94%E0%B8%9E%E0%B8%A3%E0%B8%B0%E0%B8%A8%E0%B8%A3%E0%B8%B5%E0%B8%A3%E0%B8%B1%E0%B8%95%E0%B8%99%E0%B8%A8%E0%B8%B2%E0%B8%AA%E0%B8%94%E0%B8%B2%E0%B8%A3%E0%B8%B2%E0%B8%A1-5.jpg 1.5x" data-file-width="6596" data-file-height="3710" /></a><figcaption><a href="/wiki/Wat_Phra_Kaew" title="Wat Phra Kaew">Wat Phra Kaew</a> in the <a href="/wiki/Grand_Palace" title="Grand Palace">Grand Palace</a> is among <a href="/wiki/Bangkok" title="Bangkok">Bangkok</a>'s major tourist attractions.</figcaption></figure>
<link rel="mw-deduplicated-inline-style" href="mw-data:TemplateStyles:r1320445320" /><div role="note" class="hatnote navigation-not-searchable selfref">See also the categories <a href="/wiki/Category:Tourism_in_Asia" title="Category:Tourism in Asia">Tourism in Asia</a> and <a href="/wiki/Category:Transport_in_Asia" title="Category:Transport in Asia">Transport in Asia</a></div>
<p>In 2013, with regional tourism growing and dominated by Chinese visitors, <a href="/wiki/MasterCard" class="mw-redirect" title="MasterCard">MasterCard</a> released the Global Destination Cities Index report, in which 10 of 20 cities are Asian and Pacific cities. This was also the first time an Asian city (<a href="/wiki/Bangkok" title="Bangkok">Bangkok</a>) was in the number one spot, with 15.98 million international visitors.<sup id="cite&#95;ref-140" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-140"><span class="cite-bracket">&#91;</span>127<span class="cite-bracket">&#93;</span></a></sup>
</p>
<div style="clear:both;" class=""></div>
<div class="mw-heading mw-heading2"><h2 id="Demographics">Demographics</h2></div>
<link rel="mw-deduplicated-inline-style" href="mw-data:TemplateStyles:r1320445320" /><div role="note" class="hatnote navigation-not-searchable">Main article: <a href="/wiki/Demographics_of_Asia" title="Demographics of Asia">Demographics of Asia</a></div>
<link rel="mw-deduplicated-inline-style" href="mw-data:TemplateStyles:r1320445320" /><div role="note" class="hatnote navigation-not-searchable">See also: <a href="/wiki/List_of_Asian_countries_by_population" title="List of Asian countries by population">List of Asian countries by population</a> and <a href="/wiki/List_of_Asian_countries_by_life_expectancy" title="List of Asian countries by life expectancy">List of Asian countries by life expectancy</a></div>

class="cite-bracket">&#91;</span>2<span class="cite-bracket">&#93;</span></a></sup><sup id="cite&#95;ref-UN&#95;WPP&#95;2022&#95;3-2" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-UN_WPP_2022-3"><span <figure class="mw-default-size" typeof="mw:File/Thumb"><a href="/wiki/File:WorldPopulation.png" class="mw-file-description"><img src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/6/61/WorldPopulation.png/250px-WorldPopulation.png" decoding="async" width="250" height="182" class="mw-file-element" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/6/61/WorldPopulation.png/500px-WorldPopulation.png 1.5x" data-file-width="916" data-file-height="666" /></a><figcaption>World population</figcaption></figure><p> East Asia had by far the strongest overall <a href="/wiki/Human_Development_Index" title="Human Development Index">Human Development Index</a> (HDI) improvement of any region in the world, nearly doubling average HDI attainment over the past 40 years, according to the report's analysis of health, education and income data. China, the second highest achiever in the world in terms of HDI improvement since 1970, is the only country on the "Top 10 Movers" list due to income rather than health or education achievements. Its per capita income increased a stunning 21-fold over the last four decades, also lifting hundreds of millions out of income poverty. Yet it was not among the region's top performers in improving school enrollment and life expectancy.<sup id="cite&#95;ref-2010&#95;Human&#95;Development&#95;Report&#95;141-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-2010_Human_Development_Report-141"><span class="cite-bracket">&#91;</span>128<span class="cite-bracket">&#93;</span></a></sup><br /><a href="/wiki/Nepal" title="Nepal">Nepal</a>, a South Asian country, emerges as one of the world's fastest movers since 1970 mainly due to health and education achievements. Its present <a href="/wiki/Life_expectancy" title="Life expectancy">life expectancy</a> is 25 years longer than in the 1970s. More than four of every five children of school age in Nepal now attend primary school, compared to just one in five 40 years ago.<sup id="cite&#95;ref-2010&#95;Human&#95;Development&#95;Report&#95;141-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-2010_Human_Development_Report-141"><span class="cite-bracket">&#91;</span>128<span class="cite-bracket">&#93;</span></a></sup><br /> Hong Kong ranked highest among the countries grouped on the HDI (number 7 in the world, which is in the "very high human development" category), followed by Singapore (9), Japan (19) and South Korea (22). <a href="/wiki/Afghanistan" title="Afghanistan">Afghanistan</a> (155) ranked lowest amongst Asian countries out of the 169 countries assessed.<sup id="cite&#95;ref-2010&#95;Human&#95;Development&#95;Report&#95;141-2" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-2010_Human_Development_Report-141"><span class="cite-bracket">&#91;</span>128<span class="cite-bracket">&#93;</span></a></sup>
</p><div class="mw-heading mw-heading3"><h3 id="Languages">Languages</h3></div>
<link rel="mw-deduplicated-inline-style" href="mw-data:TemplateStyles:r1320445320" /><div role="note" class="hatnote navigation-not-searchable">Main article: <a href="/wiki/Languages_of_Asia" title="Languages of Asia">Languages of Asia</a></div>
<p>Asia is home to several <a href="/wiki/Language_families" class="mw-redirect" title="Language families">language families</a> and many <a href="/wiki/Language_isolate" title="Language isolate">language isolates</a>. Most Asian countries have more than one language that is natively spoken. For instance, according to <a href="/wiki/Ethnologue" title="Ethnologue">Ethnologue</a>, more than 700 languages are spoken in Indonesia, more than 400 languages spoken in India, and more than 100 are spoken in the Philippines. China has many languages and dialects in different provinces.
</p>
<div class="mw-heading mw-heading3"><h3 id="Religions">Religions</h3></div>
<link rel="mw-deduplicated-inline-style" href="mw-data:TemplateStyles:r1320445320" /><div role="note" class="hatnote navigation-not-searchable">See also: <a href="/wiki/Eastern_philosophy" title="Eastern philosophy">Eastern philosophy</a>, <a href="/wiki/Religion_in_Asia" title="Religion in Asia">Religion in Asia</a>, and <a href="/wiki/List_of_Asian_mythologies" title="List of Asian mythologies">List of Asian mythologies</a></div>
<p>Many of the world's <a href="/wiki/Major_religions" class="mw-redirect" title="Major religions">major religions</a> have their origins in Asia, including the five most practiced in the world (excluding <a href="/wiki/Irreligion" title="Irreligion">irreligion</a>), which are Christianity, Islam, Hinduism, Chinese folk religion (classified as Confucianism and Taoism), and Buddhism. Asian mythology is complex and diverse. The story of the <a href="/wiki/Great_Flood" class="mw-redirect" title="Great Flood">Great Flood</a> for example, as presented to Jews in the <a href="/wiki/Hebrew_Bible" title="Hebrew Bible">Hebrew Bible</a> in the narrative of <a href="/wiki/Noah" title="Noah">Noah</a>—and later to Christians in the <a href="/wiki/Old_Testament" title="Old Testament">Old Testament</a>, and to Muslims in the <a href="/wiki/Quran" title="Quran">Quran</a>—is earliest found in <a href="/wiki/Mesopotamian_mythology" title="Mesopotamian mythology">Mesopotamian mythology</a>, in the <a href="/wiki/En%C3%BBma_Eli%C5%A1" class="mw-redirect" title="Enûma Eliš">Enûma Eliš</a> and <i><a href="/wiki/Epic_of_Gilgamesh" title="Epic of Gilgamesh">Epic of Gilgamesh</a></i>. <a href="/wiki/Hindu_mythology" title="Hindu mythology">Hindu mythology</a> similarly tells about an <a href="/wiki/Avatar" title="Avatar">avatar</a> of <a href="/wiki/Vishnu" title="Vishnu">Vishnu</a> in the form of a <a href="/wiki/Matsya" title="Matsya">fish</a> who warned <a href="/wiki/Sraddhadeva_Manu" class="mw-redirect" title="Sraddhadeva Manu">Manu</a> of a terrible flood. Ancient <a href="/wiki/Chinese_mythology" title="Chinese mythology">Chinese mythology</a> also tells of a <a href="/wiki/Great_Flood_(China)" title="Great Flood (China)">Great Flood</a> spanning generations, one that required the combined efforts of emperors and divinities to control.
</p>
<div class="mw-heading mw-heading4"><h4 id="Abrahamic">Abrahamic</h4></div>
<link rel="mw-deduplicated-inline-style" href="mw-data:TemplateStyles:r1320445320" /><div role="note" class="hatnote navigation-not-searchable">See also: <a href="/wiki/Christianity_in_Asia" title="Christianity in Asia">Christianity in Asia</a> and <a href="/wiki/Islam_in_Asia" title="Islam in Asia">Islam in Asia</a></div>
<figure class="mw-default-size" typeof="mw:File/Thumb"><a href="/wiki/File:Westernwall2.jpg" class="mw-file-description"><img src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/1/17/Westernwall2.jpg/250px-Westernwall2.jpg" decoding="async" width="250" height="188" class="mw-file-element" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/1/17/Westernwall2.jpg/500px-Westernwall2.jpg 1.5x" data-file-width="2592" data-file-height="1944" /></a><figcaption>The <a href="/wiki/Western_Wall" title="Western Wall">Western Wall</a> and the <a href="/wiki/Dome_of_the_Rock" title="Dome of the Rock">Dome of the Rock</a>, <a href="/wiki/Jerusalem" title="Jerusalem">Jerusalem</a></figcaption></figure>
<figure class="mw-default-size" typeof="mw:File/Thumb"><a href="/wiki/File:Church_of_the_Nativity_(7703592746).jpg" class="mw-file-description"><img src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/6/61/Church_of_the_Nativity_%287703592746%29.jpg/250px-Church_of_the_Nativity_%287703592746%29.jpg" decoding="async" width="250" height="166" class="mw-file-element" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/6/61/Church_of_the_Nativity_%287703592746%29.jpg/500px-Church_of_the_Nativity_%287703592746%29.jpg 1.5x" data-file-width="4288" data-file-height="2848" /></a><figcaption>The <a href="/wiki/Church_of_the_Nativity" title="Church of the Nativity">Church of the Nativity</a> in <a href="/wiki/Bethlehem" title="Bethlehem">Bethlehem</a></figcaption></figure>
<figure class="mw-default-size" typeof="mw:File/Thumb"><a href="/wiki/File:Kaaba_mirror_edit_jj.jpg" class="mw-file-description"><img src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/f/f3/Kaaba_mirror_edit_jj.jpg/250px-Kaaba_mirror_edit_jj.jpg" decoding="async" width="250" height="188" class="mw-file-element" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/f/f3/Kaaba_mirror_edit_jj.jpg/500px-Kaaba_mirror_edit_jj.jpg 1.5x" data-file-width="1200" data-file-height="900" /></a><figcaption>Pilgrims in the annual <a href="/wiki/Hajj" title="Hajj">Hajj</a> at the <a href="/wiki/Kaabah" class="mw-redirect" title="Kaabah">Kaabah</a> in <a href="/wiki/Mecca" title="Mecca">Mecca</a></figcaption></figure>
<p>The <a href="/wiki/Abrahamic_religions" title="Abrahamic religions">Abrahamic religions</a> including <a href="/wiki/Judaism" title="Judaism">Judaism</a>, <a href="/wiki/Christianity" title="Christianity">Christianity</a>, <a href="/wiki/Islam" title="Islam">Islam</a>, <a href="/wiki/Druze_faith" class="mw-redirect" title="Druze faith">Druze faith</a>,<sup id="cite&#95;ref-142" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-142"><span class="cite-bracket">&#91;</span>129<span class="cite-bracket">&#93;</span></a></sup> and <a href="/wiki/Bah%C3%A1%CA%BC%C3%AD_Faith" title="Baháʼí Faith">Baháʼí Faith</a> originated in West Asia.<sup id="cite&#95;ref-143" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-143"><span class="cite-bracket">&#91;</span>130<span class="cite-bracket">&#93;</span></a></sup><sup id="cite&#95;ref-144" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-144"><span class="cite-bracket">&#91;</span>131<span class="cite-bracket">&#93;</span></a></sup>
</p><p><a href="/wiki/Judaism" title="Judaism">Judaism</a>, the oldest of the Abrahamic faiths, is practiced primarily in <a href="/wiki/Israel" title="Israel">Israel</a>, the indigenous homeland and historical birthplace of the <a href="/wiki/Jews" title="Jews">Hebrew nation</a>: which today consists both of those <a href="/wiki/Jews" title="Jews">Jews</a> who remained in <a href="/wiki/Mizrahi_Jews" title="Mizrahi Jews">the Middle East</a> and those who returned from <a href="/wiki/Jewish_diaspora" title="Jewish diaspora">diaspora</a> in Europe, North America, and other regions;<sup id="cite&#95;ref-145" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-145"><span class="cite-bracket">&#91;</span>132<span class="cite-bracket">&#93;</span></a></sup> though various diaspora communities persist worldwide. Jews are the predominant ethnic group in <a href="/wiki/Israel" title="Israel">Israel</a> (75.6%) numbering at about 6.1&#160;million,<sup id="cite&#95;ref-146" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-146"><span class="cite-bracket">&#91;</span>133<span class="cite-bracket">&#93;</span></a></sup> although the levels of adherence to Jewish religion vary. Outside of Israel there are small ancient Jewish communities in <a href="/wiki/Turkey" title="Turkey">Turkey</a> (17,400),<sup id="cite&#95;ref-147" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-147"><span class="cite-bracket">&#91;</span>134<span class="cite-bracket">&#93;</span></a></sup> <a href="/wiki/Azerbaijan" title="Azerbaijan">Azerbaijan</a> (9,100),<sup id="cite&#95;ref-148" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-148"><span class="cite-bracket">&#91;</span>135<span class="cite-bracket">&#93;</span></a></sup> Iran (8,756),<sup id="cite&#95;ref-149" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-149"><span class="cite-bracket">&#91;</span>136<span class="cite-bracket">&#93;</span></a></sup> India (5,000) and <a href="/wiki/Uzbekistan" title="Uzbekistan">Uzbekistan</a> (4,000),<sup id="cite&#95;ref-150" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-150"><span class="cite-bracket">&#91;</span>137<span class="cite-bracket">&#93;</span></a></sup> among many other places. As of 2016, there are am estimated 14.4–17.5&#160;million (2016, est.)<sup id="cite&#95;ref-151" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-151"><span class="cite-bracket">&#91;</span>138<span class="cite-bracket">&#93;</span></a></sup> Jews alive in the world today, making them one of the smallest Asian minorities, at roughly 0.3–0.4&amp; of the total population of the continent.
</p><p><a href="/wiki/Christianity" title="Christianity">Christianity</a> is a widespread religion in Asia, with more than 286&#160;million adherents in 2010 according to <a href="/wiki/Pew_Research_Center" title="Pew Research Center">Pew Research Center</a>,<sup id="cite&#95;ref-152" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-152"><span class="cite-bracket">&#91;</span>139<span class="cite-bracket">&#93;</span></a></sup> and nearly 364&#160;million according to <a href="/wiki/Britannica" class="mw-redirect" title="Britannica">Britannica</a> Book of the Year 2014.<sup id="cite&#95;ref-153" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-153"><span class="cite-bracket">&#91;</span>140<span class="cite-bracket">&#93;</span></a></sup> Christians constitute around 12.6% of the total population of Asia. In the Philippines and <a href="/wiki/Timor-Leste" title="Timor-Leste">Timor-Leste</a>, <a href="/wiki/Catholicism" class="mw-redirect" title="Catholicism">Catholicism</a> is the predominant religion;<sup id="cite&#95;ref-Pew&#95;Research&#95;Center&#95;154-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Pew_Research_Center-154"><span class="cite-bracket">&#91;</span>141<span class="cite-bracket">&#93;</span></a></sup> it was introduced by the Spaniards and the Portuguese, respectively. In <a href="/wiki/Armenia" title="Armenia">Armenia</a> and Georgia, <a href="/wiki/Eastern_Orthodoxy" title="Eastern Orthodoxy">Eastern Orthodoxy</a> is the predominant religion.<sup id="cite&#95;ref-Pew&#95;Research&#95;Center&#95;154-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Pew_Research_Center-154"><span class="cite-bracket">&#91;</span>141<span class="cite-bracket">&#93;</span></a></sup> In the Middle East, such as in the <a href="/wiki/Levant" title="Levant">Levant</a>, <a href="/wiki/Anatolia" title="Anatolia">Anatolia</a> and <a href="/wiki/Fars_(territory)" title="Fars (territory)">Fars</a>, <a href="/wiki/Syriac_Christianity" title="Syriac Christianity">Syriac Christianity</a> (<a href="/wiki/Church_of_the_East" title="Church of the East">Church of the East</a>) and <a href="/wiki/Oriental_Orthodoxy" class="mw-redirect" title="Oriental Orthodoxy">Oriental Orthodoxy</a> are prevalent minority denominations,<sup id="cite&#95;ref-155" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-155"><span class="cite-bracket">&#91;</span>142<span class="cite-bracket">&#93;</span></a></sup> which are both <a href="/wiki/Eastern_Christian" class="mw-redirect" title="Eastern Christian">Eastern Christian</a> sects mainly adhered to <a href="/wiki/Assyrian_people" class="mw-redirect" title="Assyrian people">Assyrian people</a> or Syriac Christians. Vibrant indigenous minorities in <a href="/wiki/West_Asia" title="West Asia">West Asia</a> are adhering to the <a href="/wiki/Eastern_Catholic_Churches" title="Eastern Catholic Churches">Eastern Catholic Churches</a> and <a href="/wiki/Eastern_Orthodoxy" title="Eastern Orthodoxy">Eastern Orthodoxy</a>.<sup id="cite&#95;ref-Pew&#95;Research&#95;Center&#95;154-2" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Pew_Research_Center-154"><span class="cite-bracket">&#91;</span>141<span class="cite-bracket">&#93;</span></a></sup> <a href="/wiki/Saint_Thomas_Christians" title="Saint Thomas Christians">Saint Thomas Christians</a> in India trace their origins to the evangelistic activity of <a href="/wiki/Thomas_the_Apostle" title="Thomas the Apostle">Thomas the Apostle</a> in the 1st century.<sup id="cite&#95;ref-156" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-156"><span class="cite-bracket">&#91;</span>143<span class="cite-bracket">&#93;</span></a></sup> Significant Christian communities also found in <a href="/wiki/Central_Asia" title="Central Asia">Central Asia</a>, <a href="/wiki/South_Asia" title="South Asia">South Asia</a>, <a href="/wiki/Southeast_Asia" title="Southeast Asia">Southeast Asia</a> and <a href="/wiki/East_Asia" title="East Asia">East Asia</a>.<sup id="cite&#95;ref-Pew&#95;Research&#95;Center&#95;154-3" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Pew_Research_Center-154"><span class="cite-bracket">&#91;</span>141<span class="cite-bracket">&#93;</span></a></sup>
</p><p><a href="/wiki/Islam" title="Islam">Islam</a>, which originated in the <a href="/wiki/Hejaz" title="Hejaz">Hejaz</a> located in modern-day Saudi Arabia, is the second largest and most widely-spread religion in Asia with at least 1&#160;billion Muslims constituting around 23.8% of the total population of Asia.<sup id="cite&#95;ref-157" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-157"><span class="cite-bracket">&#91;</span>144<span class="cite-bracket">&#93;</span></a></sup> With 12.7% of the world Muslim population, the country currently with the largest Muslim population in the world is Indonesia, followed by Pakistan (11.5%), India (10%), Bangladesh, Iran and Turkey. <a href="/wiki/Mecca" title="Mecca">Mecca</a>, <a href="/wiki/Medina" title="Medina">Medina</a> and <a href="/wiki/Holiest_sites_in_Islam#Jerusalem" title="Holiest sites in Islam">Jerusalem</a> are the three holiest cities for Islam in all the world. The <a href="/wiki/Hajj" title="Hajj">Hajj</a> and <a href="/wiki/Umrah" title="Umrah">Umrah</a> attract large numbers of Muslim devotees from all over the world to Mecca and Medina. Iran is the largest <a href="/wiki/Shia" class="mw-redirect" title="Shia">Shia</a> country.
</p><p>The <a href="/wiki/Druze" title="Druze">Druze</a> originated in West Asia, is a monotheistic religion based on the teachings of figures like <a href="/wiki/Hamza_ibn_Ali" title="Hamza ibn Ali">Hamza ibn Ali</a> and <a href="/wiki/Al-Hakim_bi-Amr_Allah" title="Al-Hakim bi-Amr Allah">al-Hakim bi-Amr Allah</a>, and Greek <a href="/wiki/Philosophers" class="mw-redirect" title="Philosophers">philosophers</a> such as <a href="/wiki/Plato" title="Plato">Plato</a> and <a href="/wiki/Aristotle" title="Aristotle">Aristotle</a>. The number of <a href="/wiki/Druze" title="Druze">Druze</a> people worldwide is around one million. Around 45–50% live in <a href="/wiki/Syria" title="Syria">Syria</a>, 35% to 40% live in <a href="/wiki/Lebanon" title="Lebanon">Lebanon</a>, and less than 10% live in <a href="/wiki/Israel" title="Israel">Israel</a>. Recently there has been a growing Druze diaspora.<sup id="cite&#95;ref-158" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-158"><span class="cite-bracket">&#91;</span>145<span class="cite-bracket">&#93;</span></a></sup>
</p><p>The <a href="/wiki/Bah%C3%A1%CA%BC%C3%AD_Faith" title="Baháʼí Faith">Baháʼí Faith</a> originated in Asia, in Iran (Persia), and spread from there to the Ottoman Empire, Central Asia, India, and Burma during the lifetime of <a href="/wiki/Bah%C3%A1%27u%27ll%C3%A1h" class="mw-redirect" title="Bahá&#39;u&#39;lláh">Bahá'u'lláh</a>. Since the middle of the 20th century, growth has particularly occurred in other Asian countries, because Baháʼí activities in many Muslim countries has been <a href="/wiki/Persecution_of_Bah%C3%A1%CA%BC%C3%ADs" title="Persecution of Baháʼís">severely suppressed</a> by authorities. <a href="/wiki/Lotus_Temple" title="Lotus Temple">Lotus Temple</a> is a big <a href="/wiki/Bah%C3%A1%CA%BC%C3%AD_temple" class="mw-redirect" title="Baháʼí temple">Baháʼí temple</a> in India.
</p>
<div class="mw-heading mw-heading4"><h4 id="Indian_and_East_Asian_religions">Indian and East Asian religions</h4></div>
<link rel="mw-deduplicated-inline-style" href="mw-data:TemplateStyles:r1320445320" /><div role="note" class="hatnote navigation-not-searchable">See also: <a href="/wiki/Eastern_religions" title="Eastern religions">Eastern religions</a></div><figure class="mw-default-size" typeof="mw:File/Thumb"><a href="/wiki/File:Akshardham_Lotus.jpg" class="mw-file-description"><img src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/a/a4/Akshardham_Lotus.jpg/250px-Akshardham_Lotus.jpg" decoding="async" width="250" height="188" class="mw-file-element" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/a/a4/Akshardham_Lotus.jpg 1.5x" data-file-width="435" data-file-height="327" /></a><figcaption>The <a href="/wiki/Swaminarayan_Hinduism" class="mw-redirect" title="Swaminarayan Hinduism">Swaminarayan</a> <a href="/wiki/Akshardham_Temple" class="mw-redirect" title="Akshardham Temple">Akshardham Temple</a> in <a href="/wiki/Delhi" title="Delhi">Delhi</a>, according to the <a href="/wiki/Guinness_World_Record" class="mw-redirect" title="Guinness World Record">Guinness World Records</a>, is the <i>World's Largest Comprehensive Hindu Temple</i>.<sup id="cite&#95;ref-159" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-159"><span class="cite-bracket">&#91;</span>146<span class="cite-bracket">&#93;</span></a></sup></figcaption></figure>
<p>Almost all Asian religions have philosophical character and Asian philosophical traditions cover a large spectrum of philosophical thoughts and writings. <a href="/wiki/Indian_philosophy" title="Indian philosophy">Indian philosophy</a> includes <a href="/wiki/Hindu_philosophy" title="Hindu philosophy">Hindu philosophy</a> and <a href="/wiki/Buddhist_philosophy" title="Buddhist philosophy">Buddhist philosophy</a>. They include elements of nonmaterial pursuits, whereas another school of thought from India, <a href="/wiki/C%C4%81rv%C4%81ka" class="mw-redirect" title="Cārvāka">Cārvāka</a>, preached the enjoyment of the material world. The religions of <a href="/wiki/Hinduism" title="Hinduism">Hinduism</a>, <a href="/wiki/Buddhism" title="Buddhism">Buddhism</a>, <a href="/wiki/Jainism" title="Jainism">Jainism</a> and <a href="/wiki/Sikhism" title="Sikhism">Sikhism</a> originated in India, South Asia. In East Asia, particularly in China and Japan, <a href="/wiki/Confucianism" title="Confucianism">Confucianism</a>, <a href="/wiki/Taoism" title="Taoism">Taoism</a> and <a href="/wiki/Zen" title="Zen">Zen</a> Buddhism took shape.
</p><p>As of 2012<sup class="plainlinks noexcerpt noprint asof-tag update" style="display:none;"><a class="external text" href="https://en.wikipedia.org/w/index.php?title=Asia&amp;action=edit">&#91;update&#93;</a></sup>, Hinduism has around 1.1&#160;billion adherents. The faith represents around 25% of Asia's population and is the largest religion in Asia. However, it is mostly concentrated in South Asia. Over 80% of the populations of both India and Nepal adhere to Hinduism, alongside significant communities in Bangladesh, Pakistan, Bhutan, Sri Lanka and <a href="/wiki/Bali" title="Bali">Bali</a>, Indonesia. Many overseas Indians in countries such as Burma, Singapore and Malaysia also adhere to Hinduism.
</p>
<figure class="mw-default-size" typeof="mw:File/Thumb"><a href="/wiki/File:Angkor_Wat_reflejado_en_un_estanque_02.jpg" class="mw-file-description"><img src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/e/ef/Angkor_Wat_reflejado_en_un_estanque_02.jpg/250px-Angkor_Wat_reflejado_en_un_estanque_02.jpg" decoding="async" width="250" height="165" class="mw-file-element" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/e/ef/Angkor_Wat_reflejado_en_un_estanque_02.jpg/500px-Angkor_Wat_reflejado_en_un_estanque_02.jpg 1.5x" data-file-width="3928" data-file-height="2600" /></a><figcaption>The Angkor Wat in Cambodia, a Hindu-Buddhist temple and the largest religious monument in the world</figcaption></figure>
<p>Buddhism has a great following in mainland Southeast Asia and East Asia. Buddhism is the religion of the majority of the populations of Cambodia (96%),<sup id="cite&#95;ref-160" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-160"><span class="cite-bracket">&#91;</span>147<span class="cite-bracket">&#93;</span></a></sup> <a href="/wiki/Thailand" title="Thailand">Thailand</a> (95%),<sup id="cite&#95;ref-161" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-161"><span class="cite-bracket">&#91;</span>148<span class="cite-bracket">&#93;</span></a></sup> <a href="/wiki/Burma" class="mw-redirect" title="Burma">Burma</a> (80–89%),<sup id="cite&#95;ref-162" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-162"><span class="cite-bracket">&#91;</span>149<span class="cite-bracket">&#93;</span></a></sup> Japan (36–96%),<sup id="cite&#95;ref-163" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-163"><span class="cite-bracket">&#91;</span>150<span class="cite-bracket">&#93;</span></a></sup> <a href="/wiki/Bhutan" title="Bhutan">Bhutan</a> (75–84%),<sup id="cite&#95;ref-164" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-164"><span class="cite-bracket">&#91;</span>151<span class="cite-bracket">&#93;</span></a></sup> <a href="/wiki/Sri_Lanka" title="Sri Lanka">Sri Lanka</a> (70%),<sup id="cite&#95;ref-165" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-165"><span class="cite-bracket">&#91;</span>152<span class="cite-bracket">&#93;</span></a></sup> <a href="/wiki/Laos" title="Laos">Laos</a> (60–67%)<sup id="cite&#95;ref-166" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-166"><span class="cite-bracket">&#91;</span>153<span class="cite-bracket">&#93;</span></a></sup> and <a href="/wiki/Mongolia" title="Mongolia">Mongolia</a> (53–93%).<sup id="cite&#95;ref-167" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-167"><span class="cite-bracket">&#91;</span>154<span class="cite-bracket">&#93;</span></a></sup> <a href="/wiki/Taiwan" title="Taiwan">Taiwan</a> (35–93%),<sup id="cite&#95;ref-168" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-168"><span class="cite-bracket">&#91;</span>155<span class="cite-bracket">&#93;</span></a></sup><sup id="cite&#95;ref-169" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-169"><span class="cite-bracket">&#91;</span>156<span class="cite-bracket">&#93;</span></a></sup><sup id="cite&#95;ref-170" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-170"><span class="cite-bracket">&#91;</span>157<span class="cite-bracket">&#93;</span></a></sup><sup id="cite&#95;ref-171" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-171"><span class="cite-bracket">&#91;</span>158<span class="cite-bracket">&#93;</span></a></sup> South Korea (23–50%),<sup id="cite&#95;ref-172" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-172"><span class="cite-bracket">&#91;</span>159<span class="cite-bracket">&#93;</span></a></sup> <a href="/wiki/Malaysia" title="Malaysia">Malaysia</a> (19–21%),<sup id="cite&#95;ref-173" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-173"><span class="cite-bracket">&#91;</span>160<span class="cite-bracket">&#93;</span></a></sup> <a href="/wiki/Nepal" title="Nepal">Nepal</a> (9–11%),<sup id="cite&#95;ref-174" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-174"><span class="cite-bracket">&#91;</span>161<span class="cite-bracket">&#93;</span></a></sup> <a href="/wiki/Vietnam" title="Vietnam">Vietnam</a> (10–75%),<sup id="cite&#95;ref-175" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-175"><span class="cite-bracket">&#91;</span>162<span class="cite-bracket">&#93;</span></a></sup> China (20–50%),<sup id="cite&#95;ref-176" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-176"><span class="cite-bracket">&#91;</span>163<span class="cite-bracket">&#93;</span></a></sup> <a href="/wiki/North_Korea" title="North Korea">North Korea</a> (2–14%),<sup id="cite&#95;ref-177" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-177"><span class="cite-bracket">&#91;</span>164<span class="cite-bracket">&#93;</span></a></sup><sup id="cite&#95;ref-178" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-178"><span class="cite-bracket">&#91;</span>165<span class="cite-bracket">&#93;</span></a></sup><sup id="cite&#95;ref-179" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-179"><span class="cite-bracket">&#91;</span>166<span class="cite-bracket">&#93;</span></a></sup> and small communities in India and Bangladesh. The Communist-governed countries of China, Vietnam and North Korea are officially atheist, thus the number of Buddhists and other religious adherents may be under-reported.
</p><p><a href="/wiki/Jainism" title="Jainism">Jainism</a> is found mainly in India and in overseas Indian communities such as the United States and Malaysia. <a href="/wiki/Sikhism" title="Sikhism">Sikhism</a> is found in Northern India and amongst overseas Indian communities in other parts of Asia, especially Southeast Asia. <a href="/wiki/Confucianism" title="Confucianism">Confucianism</a> is found predominantly in mainland China, South Korea, Taiwan and in overseas Chinese populations. <a href="/wiki/Taoism" title="Taoism">Taoism</a> is found mainly in mainland China, Taiwan, Malaysia and Singapore. In many Chinese communities, Taoism is easily syncretised with <a href="/wiki/Mahayana_Buddhism" class="mw-redirect" title="Mahayana Buddhism">Mahayana Buddhism</a>, thus exact religious statistics are difficult to obtain and may be understated or overstated.
</p>
